# Changelog

**What this file is: the repository's dated engineering journal, newest entry
first.** One entry per significant change anywhere in this repository — engine,
gem, demo, deploy runbook — saying what was wrong, what changed, and the intent,
written for someone who was not there. Entries are as long as that takes.
MEASURED 2026-09-06 over the 461 top-level entries then in the file: median 776
characters, p90 1,329, longest 4,350, and **20 of them — four per cent — short
enough (≤250 characters) to be the «one line per change, 1–2 sentences» the header
used to ask for.** A header its own file contradicts on ninety-six per cent of its
lines teaches a reader to skip headers, so it says what is true instead.

**Every top-level entry opens with its ISO date, and that is held.** It is this
file's only ordering affordance — nothing else here says when — so
`bin/check-changelog` fails on an entry that omits one (arm CL-7). Two entries
had lost theirs when the arm was written; they were dated from the commits that
introduced them, and nothing else about either entry was touched.

**The short per-release record lives with the package it describes.** Each gem's
own `<gem>/CHANGELOG.md` is Keep-a-Changelog, one line per change under the
release it shipped in; it is inside the published `.gem` and is what a reader who
installed the gem and never saw this repository has. A change to a gem's
published surface gets its release note there as well as its entry here. **That
short form is the INTENT those files are written to, not a claim about every
entry already in them (K-1382)** — measured across all 14 tracked gem changelogs,
189 entries, half of them are at or under 250 characters, but
`kiosk-server/CHANGELOG.md` is 70 of those entries on its own and keeps it on 12,
at median 629, which is this file's register rather than a release note's. It is
not repaired by rewriting them: a changelog is append-only. The next entry is
where the form is kept.

**Nothing already written is edited.** History is append-only: an entry that has
turned out to be wrong is superseded by a new entry that says so and names it,
never rewritten. Several entries below are corrections of exactly that kind.

- 2026-09-08: **this repository's own constitution still stated the changelog rule this file's header was corrected away from, so a wave had two rules for one file (K-1419).** `reference/CLAUDE.md` rule 5 required «ONE line: 1–2 sentences» for a repository-root entry, which K-1342 measured to have held on four per cent of 461 entries here, and which the header above has said the opposite of since. A wave reading the sibling wrote short entries and a wave reading the umbrella wrote journal entries, and nothing reddened on either. Rule 5 in both sibling constitutions now says what the umbrella says: the concise form is the INTENT of a per-gem release note, which ships inside the package and is met with no repository around it, while a repository-root changelog is a dated engineering journal. Intent: one file, one rule, and the numbers derived rather than quoted.

- 2026-09-08: **getgrocery published one delivery window in two spellings across four verbs, and a README promised the spelling one of them did not send (K-1424, K-1425).** `delivery_slots` offered a window with a zone-bearing label beside the instant and `my_orders` read it back the same way, but `create_order` answered the instant alone and `reschedule_delivery` answered a bare `rescheduled_at` with nothing beside it — while the README stated the rule for all of them, one commit before the code met it. An instant with no clock named beside it is the same instant in a second spelling, and `my_orders` is the verb §11.6 sends an assistant to after a lost `pay`, so it is the row most likely to be read back to a human out loud. Both write verbs now publish the label from the ONE writer the read verbs use (`DeliverySlots.label`), declared and exampled in their own output schemas, and the README names all four. Intent: an assistant that books, reschedules and reconciles hears one window said one way.

- 2026-09-08: **three shipped comments taught a false mechanism for how a `timestamptz` comes back, and a fourth in the same codebase taught the true one (K-1426).** getgrocery's `DeliverySlots.label` and two comments on its admin orders page attributed the returned clock to the DATABASE CONNECTION — one of them saying so in terms, «because no `SET LOCAL` has been issued». Measured: no zone-related `SET LOCAL` is issued anywhere in the tree and `config.time_zone` is commented out, so ActiveRecord hands the value back as a TimeWithZone in `Time.zone`, which is UTC here. The output was right in every case; what was wrong was the model the next maintainer would edit against, and the codebase said both things about one behaviour. The three now state the mechanism the code has. Intent: a comment explains the code, and a comment that is confidently wrong is worse than none.

- 2026-09-08: **atablefor's `publish_instant` was documented as the single publication point for a booking instant and was one of three (K-1423).** `my_bookings` went through the helper; `availability` and the `book_table` confirmation each called `.iso8601` on a Seatings-zoned Time directly. The bytes agreed, so no wire answer was wrong — but the sentence a reader relies on to know whether the NEXT edit is safe was false, and the helper exists precisely so that a change to the published form reaches every verb at once. All three now go through it and the comment names all three. Intent: the one-writer claim is a property of the code rather than of its documentation.

- 2026-09-08: **four demo descriptors still spent an assistant's context restating shape their own schemas declare (K-1416).** The residue of the same ADR-0023 sweep that corrected hoteling: getgrocery's catalogue prose gave the price unit and currency that `price_cents` and `currency` already carry, skooti's fleet prose did the same and additionally announced «takes no parameters» three lines above the empty closed `input_schema` that publishes exactly that, stylish marked a parameter «Optional» where the `required` list two declarations down already says so, and philslist's browse verb carried the same «every filter is optional» clause that was removed from hoteling. Each keeps the SEMANTICS no schema can hold — that a cart is signed at the operator-quoted total, that filters AND together, that an empty array means nothing matched — and drops the restatement. Intent: every restated byte is spent out of an assistant's context before it can call anything.

- 2026-09-08: **twelve of the fifteen `gem` lines in the gem READMEs did not resolve, under a note promising that they would (K-1427).** Each of eleven README install banners says «until they are published, every `gem` line below needs `github: "kiosk-hq/kiosk"`» — and, measured, only three lines carried it: the monorepo README's and the two under kiosk-server's «The line» heading. So an adopter copying any of the other twelve got a line bundler cannot resolve until publication, and nothing anywhere held either behaviour, which was proved by planting the qualifier's removal and watching five guards stay green. All fifteen now carry it and the banner states what the block DOES rather than instructing the reader to fix it. Intent: a snippet in an install section is copy-pasteable or it is not a snippet.

- 2026-09-08: **a guard's green line called a `get`-routed read verb a «worked write verb» (K-1421), and a rewritten cursor helper still documented itself as base64 (K-1403).** Two sentences about code, in the files that code lives in. `check-onboarding-derivation`'s PAST_WINDOW rule prints the verbs that refuse a stale delivery window; its subject set is right and includes getgrocery's `delivery_slots`, which the origin's route table draws with `get`, so the rule now names no HTTP method at all and says why. And `Cursor`'s class comment 29 lines above the paragraph that publishes the offset in clear still told a reader the helper is base64 — the one sentence the K-1403 fix rewrote the file around and did not touch. Intent: a guard's self-description is read by whoever is deciding whether its green line covers their change, and a stale one costs exactly that reader.

- 2026-09-08: **two public documents still narrated a retired element inside the declared scope of the sweep that was supposed to clear them (K-1409).** philslist's README carried the byte-twin of a stylish passage about a deleted `stub_idp.rb` / `jwt_or_stub_idp.rb` pair — kept in stylish because it is a claims row's exemption evidence by name, an exemption that does not transfer — and `deploy/README.md` described the edge rate limit in the past tense although the `(ratelimit)` snippet is still shipped, commented, for an operator to reach for. philslist now says what its identity seam IS, and the deploy note states what the shipped snippet DOES, which is the stronger sentence in both cases. Intent: a reader arriving today has no memory to correct.

- 2026-09-08: **the engine's route table told an implementer the wrong status for two of the three unconditional routes it draws, and a spec had written the contradiction down rather than repairing it (K-1436).** `POST pay` and `POST agents/kyc` are drawn on every mounted host, and the comments beside them promised the wire's own **403** where a host configures no `payment_provider` or no `kyc_public_key`. Both refusals raise `Errors::ModuleNotServed`, whose `HTTP_STATUS` is **501**, and the raise sites say why in as many words: whether this origin does payments (or KYC) at all is a fact about the ORIGIN and true of every caller, while `forbidden` means «authenticated, but this identity may not do this». The route comments now name `module_not_served` (501) and carry that reason, so the table an implementer reads agrees with the wire they will meet. `executor_spec.rb` had a parenthesis reading «the code the route comment calls "the wire's own 403" is `module_not_served` at 501» — a spec correcting the shipped comment instead of the comment being fixed — and it goes with the sentence it was correcting. **CORRECTION TO THIS FILE'S OWN 2026-09-08 T-183 ENTRY**, which repeats the 403 in the clause about philslist and tudu gaining `POST /kiosk/pay`: what those two answer is `module_not_served` at 501. The entry is left standing, as this file's rule requires, and this sentence supersedes that clause.

- 2026-09-08: **the wire error vocabulary was stated three hundred lines apart with two different sizes, and the low one is the number a client author would have believed (K-1437).** `errors.rb`'s header says «these seventeen codes» twice; a comment inside `to_problem` said an assistant «matches the same fifteen strings it always did» — the count from before `verb_not_found` and `module_not_served` existed. Seventeen is right: `Errors::CODES` carries 17 and the published `problem.schema.json` enum carries the same 17, which the suite already derives one from the other. A reader who trusted fifteen ships a client with no branch for two live codes. The sentence is corrected AND the class is closed: a new example reads every English number-word in `errors.rb`'s own comments and holds each to `CODES.size`, with a vacuity arm that fails when no comment states the size at all — so the next code added to the vocabulary reddens the file that describes it. (The 16 exception subclasses are a third, correct number: `quota_exceeded` has no class, which the file already says.)

- 2026-09-08: **three shipped comments in the engine described a flag, a list and a section as something other than what they are (K-1438, K-1439).** `configuration_extension.rb`'s banner called request-shape validation «opt-in» ten lines above the paragraph explaining why it DEFAULTS TRUE, and tagged it with a decision name that resolves in three private files and nowhere in either public repository — an adopter reading the gem met a label with no referent. `request_validation.rb` said «Opt-in» for the same flag in its own first line. `open_api.rb` headed a five-item list «the four things this document must get right» and numbered the items 1, 2, 3, 5, 4, so a reader following the numbers read item 5 before item 4 and could not tell whether one was missing. All three now say what is: the flag is on by default and the prose says how to turn it off, the list says five and reads in order, and the memo section's banner names the HTTP CACHE validator it is actually about rather than leaving «validator» to collide with the schema validators the same file discusses.

- 2026-09-08: **twelve lines of quoted Russian and six files naming the maintainer shipped inside the engine a stranger installs, and no gate could see either (K-1440).** Measured over `kiosk-server/lib`: 12 Cyrillic lines across `headers.rb`, `discovery_controller.rb`, `schema_document.rb`, `schema_slots.rb` and `open_api.rb`, every one a quotation from a design conversation, plus «the maintainer accepted this control's weakness openly» in `engine.rb`. This is the T-186 class one layer worse than a ledger id: a reader who cannot read the sentence cannot even tell whether it binds them, and an attribution asks a stranger to trust a person instead of reading a property. Every one now says the same thing in English as a statement about the code — why sixty seconds is the post-deploy staleness this wire serves, why a public document sets no `Vary`, why the catalogue must publish a new category without a deploy, why a log line is a weak control. `bin/check-shipped-archaeology` gains R3 (no Cyrillic, in the gems and the demos alike) and R4 (no maintainer named in prose, gems only — one demo is an app named after him), each with planted-RED arms, a carve-out arm for the `spec.authors` line every gemspec must carry, and an arm proving R3 does not fire on guillemets, em dashes, § or accented Latin. Specs are deliberately out of scope, on T-186's declared developer-surface exemption.

- 2026-09-08: **the demos stop citing a private ledger and private ADRs anywhere an operator reads, and the invariant that used to exempt comments now covers them (K-1435, Phil: «убери из демок ВСЮ археологию»).** `bin/check-demo-copies` banned a ledger id in the demo tree's CODE TOKENS — a `desc`, a banner, an `abort` — and left comments alone, on the argument that a comment is a note to the next maintainer. That argument does not survive the demos being the first thing an operator opens: somebody evaluating Kiosk reads `kiosk-demo-hoteling/app` to find out what adopting it looks like. The exempt half was measured regrowing — 24 comment-carried occurrences of 9 ids, then 41 of 17 one day later, all of it by ordinary fix waves rather than by anyone deciding to. 34 ledger citations and 25 ADR references are gone from eight demos: a provenance cite dropped where the sentence stands without it, a history clause restated as what the code IS where it did not. `bin/check-shipped-archaeology` now holds the demo tree on the same terms as the gems — comments and prose included, over `app/`, `lib/`, `script/`, `config/`, `db/`, `Rakefile`, `config.ru`, `README.md` and `before-after.md` — and `check-demo-copies` keeps its code-token rule as the narrower statement about terminals. That rule's vacuity arm counted demo comment citations as proof its pattern still worked, which stopped being evidence the moment the tree was scrubbed; it is replaced, as its own header demanded rather than deleted, by fixtures the rule owns — the shapes people write, held against the pattern, and a comment-versus-string pair held against the lexer the whole ban rests on.

- 2026-09-08: **the engine told a reader its vendored-schema sync check was «still out of scope» while that check was green in CI, and the check itself could not prove it still reddened (K-1441).** `kiosk-server/lib/kiosk/server/schemas/pow.schema.json` is a vendored copy of the normative kiosk.tech schema, and the file that names it carried a sentence sending a reader to look for a hole `bin/check-spec-schemas` had filled a fortnight earlier. The sentence now states what holds the copy and where it can run. The guard gains a `--self-test`: a planted RED arm per rule (drift from either side, a published schema vendored nowhere, a copy no longer published, a copy that stops parsing, a copy that is deleted) plus vacuity arms over the real manifest — which **found a defect on their first run**: `e2e/schemas/mandates.schema.json` had lost its `$comment` provenance marker and was the one copy of eight that did not say it was a copy. Restored. And the sibling policy is now two spellings rather than one: sibling-less it still prints NOT COMPARED and exits 0, which is right for a developer's single-repo clone, but `KIOSK_REQUIRE_SITE_SIBLING=1` makes that state a FAILURE and the workflow sets it on the one job that checks kiosk.tech out — so a checkout step that quietly produced nothing is red rather than green, the same variable and the same argument as that job's skill-pin examples.

- 2026-09-08: **the shipped gems cited a private ledger 631 times, at readers who can never open it (T-186, Phil: «Я ж просил - убери из демок ВСЮ археологию»).** A stranger who adds one of these gems to their Gemfile and opens the source met «the K-1035 class», «the T-090 rule», «ADR-0018» — inside code they are being asked to adopt. None of those ids resolves for them and none ever will: the findings ledger is private and the ADRs live in a private repository. MEASURED at head with `git grep -ohP` (PCRE, because the ERE spelling of a backslash shorthand matches nothing and exits 1): **576 ledger ids across the fourteen gems' `lib/`** — kiosk-server 473, kiosk-redteam 55, pow-equihash 14, pay-stripe 13, core 8, reputation 8, rls 3, user-idp-devise 2 — plus **53 `ADR-00NN` references** and the READMEs, gemspecs, the device-verify view and the vendored PoW schema, for **634 occurrences in 106 files**. All of them are gone.

  **The ban had to reach COMMENTS, and that is the one judgement in this change.** `bin/check-demo-copies` holds the demo tree's CODE TOKENS only, on the argument that a `desc`, a banner or an `abort` reaches an operator's terminal while a comment is a note to the next maintainer standing beside the code. That argument does not carry across: the reader of `kiosk-server/lib` IS the adopter, comments are the surface they read, and effectively all 634 were in comments. Six citations were in STRINGS and reached a screen already — three Stripe log lines an operator meets when `setup_url` degrades, two sentences a red-team battery prints, and one emitted SQL comment in the identity-table amendment — and each now states the condition instead of naming a row.

  **Stripping an id is not deleting the sentence, and three shapes were handled differently.** A provenance citation was dropped and its sentence kept. A sentence written as HISTORY was restated in the present tense — `executor.rb` now says what the three dispatcher verbs are and why `schema` is not one of them, rather than what a deleted second constant used to hold; `configuration_extension.rb` says request-shape validation defaults TRUE once, where it had contradicted itself three paragraphs apart; `mandate_verifier.rb` says the currency domain IS closed, which is what `CURRENCY_CODE` does. A comment that only narrated a decision was deleted. Nothing was left as a stub: no «see the ledger», no «per an earlier finding». Two of the deletions are the class Phil named on 2026-09-07 — the afterlife of 0.3's `POST /kiosk/query` and `POST /kiosk/run`, which survived in `engine.rb`, `wire_controller.rb`, `verb_controller.rb`, the kiosk-server README, `kiosk/protocol.rb` and, in eight copies, the `config/routes/kiosk.rb` an integrator copies. **That last one took a cross-check with it:** `check-demo-copies`' `no-multiplexed-pair` claim held those eight sentences to the protocol constant's own record of the deletion, and both ends of it were archaeology, so the claim is retired and `one-endpoint-per-verb` — one entry above it, and held by `bin/check-verb-routes` — is what a reader needs.

  **Nothing executable moved, and that was verified mechanically rather than by reading:** the non-comment Ripper token stream of all 90 touched Ruby files was compared against their parents and is identical except for the six sanctioned strings. Two kiosk-pay-stripe examples had asserted the operator log by matching the ID rather than the sentence; they now assert the sentence. Fifteen ledger and claims anchors cited the deleted comments — nine were re-pointed at the live text, and for the three whose evidence WAS the narration (`events` is removed, not stubbed) the citation became a measured absence with its tool, dialect and file set named. `spec/conformance-anchors.tsv` was regenerated.

  **The property is now held by `bin/check-shipped-archaeology`,** because nothing held it before: a planted `K-9999` in kiosk-server left check-demo-copies, check-publication-paths and check-gem-packaging all at exit 0. It bans both patterns across every non-demo gem's `lib/`, `app/`, README and gemspec, derives that scope from the layout, declares each exemption with its reason (Phil's `bin/`/`CHANGELOG.md`/`CLAUDE.md`; `spec/` and `test/`, which the TODO/FIXME rule requires to carry ids; unpackaged build files; the demo tree; and any line carrying a live TODO or FIXME), and classifies every hit with Ripper so a string and a comment get different advice. 38 self-test arms, including vacuity arms that re-run both patterns against `CHANGELOG.md` and `bin/` — outside the ban, append-only, so they cannot drain honestly. Watched red on a plant and restored from a file copy with the sha re-checked. Intent: an adopter reading this code learns the code, and is never handed a pointer to a document that is not theirs.

- 2026-09-08: **the demo live-activity telemetry is removed whole (T-191, Phil: «Убирай, это лишнее»).** It was an opt-in app-layer surface behind `KIOSK_TELEMETRY=1`: a Rack middleware recording one row per successful wire action into a `demo_telemetry_events` table, an aggregate served at `GET /demo/activity.json`, a shared hosted store provisioned by `deploy/telemetry-init.sql`, and two getgrocery rake tasks gating it — 30 tracked files carrying it and 60 referencing it, in seven demos, all for a live-activity tile that was never built on the landing page (measured with `git grep -i` at head: `kiosk.tech` has no reference to it in its working tree and none in any commit of its history, so nothing on the site ever fetched the aggregate). Everything goes: the three `app/services` files and the controller in seven demos each, the route and the middleware insertion, the migration, `demo:telemetry` and `demo:telemetry_spec`, the deploy runbook section and checklist items, and the workflow's task entries.

  THE TABLE IS RETIRED, NOT DROPPED, and the choice is the K-828 one. `bin/check-migration-replay` replays every schema vintage since the last fleet rebuild onto a scratch database, and its retired/live split is derived from head's own `db/structure.sql`: a table head no longer declares cannot be written to, so nothing inserts into it and there is no INSERT to be refused. Deleting the migration and the table's blocks from all seven tracked `db/structure.sql` therefore leaves a box provisioned before today carrying `demo_telemetry_events` forever, reported as RETIRED and declared in `ACCEPTED_LEFTOVERS` with this row — measured, 16 replays across 8 demos, exit 0, 135 leftover objects reached by the declaration. Shipping a DROP migration instead would buy nothing a demo box needs and would put a destructive statement in the adopter-facing migration history; `demo:setup` drops and recreates anyway, so every gate and every fresh install already builds from the reduced file.

  Two gates lost their subject and were re-anchored rather than deleted, because the property each held is still real. `check-demo-copies`' telemetry-wiring block asserted four pieces including the `config.autoload_once_paths` line that makes an initializer able to name an `app/services` constant; that line is still load-bearing in all seven demos (`PowDifficulty` is named from every one of them), so the rule now hangs off `app/services/pow_difficulty.rb` and its self-test arm plants the same break. `kiosk-test-support`'s SQL-splice allow-list loses its four-per-copy telemetry entry entirely, which its own floor-and-ceiling rule required be deleted in the same change. Intent: a surface nobody asked for stops being seven copies of code, a shared production database and a runbook section that an operator has to read past.

- 2026-09-08: **`bin/check-onboarding-derivation` had no rule for the half of the onboarding page that now teaches a routes file (T-184).** With the `:kiosk_verb` catch-all deleted, the published guide's step 3 has to show a `config/routes/kiosk.rb` — the engine mounted above one explicit line per verb — and a worked route table on a page is exactly the hand-typed copy this guard exists to refuse. Four rules were added, every one derived from `kiosk-demo-getgrocery`'s own `config/routes.rb` and `config/routes/kiosk.rb`, with no verb name typed anywhere in the script. `ROUTE_FILE` holds the page to the `draw(:name)` split the demo reaches its wire by, and to the path that name resolves to. `VERB_ROUTES` holds every verb the page DECLARES to the demo's own line for it — method, path and the `kiosk_verb` it defaults — and fails on a verb declared and never routed, a route naming a verb the demo does not route, a duplicate, or a method that disagrees with the kind. `MOUNT_FIRST` holds the mount line itself (so a page mounting at a hard-coded `"/kiosk"` compares unequal rather than merely present) and the fact it is drawn ABOVE every route, which is the ordering property the deleted pair had for free. `ROUTE_PATTERN` bans a dynamic segment or a route naming no verb — the shape T-183 deleted — with the demo's own static table as the live subject that makes it a ban about something.

  Each carries a planted arm and a vacuity arm, and the built-in fixture builds its own two routes blocks from the same derivations, so the sibling-less CI leg holds all 22 rules instead of going quiet on four. The page reader now hands out its Ruby blocks individually as well as concatenated: «drawn below the mount» is a question about ONE block, and the concatenation cannot tell a block from its neighbour. All five breaks were watched red on disk and restored from a file copy with the sha re-checked — a page-side route line deleted, the demo routing a query with POST, the demo's mount demoted below its routes, the demo's `draw` call removed, and a `:kiosk_verb` catch-all put back into the demo's wire file. Intent: the published guide's routes cannot drift from the origin they are copied from, in either direction.

- 2026-09-08: **a demo could lose ONE host-detection block and every guard stayed green, and the sweep that widened the subject widened the hole with it (K-1413).** `bin/check-demo-copies`' K-1353 rule holds two things — every `host = begin` block that EXISTS must agree once the demo's own vanity name is substituted, and a listed demo must have at least one — and neither sees a partial loss: measured, replacing one of stylish's six blocks with a hard-coded loopback literal left the guard at exit 0 while that one task quietly stopped telling an operator the vanity name existed. K-1400 had just taken the guarded population from twenty-five sites in four demos to forty-one in seven, so the blind spot was roughly twice as wide as when nobody had looked at it. The fix is a PAIRING rather than a count, because a per-demo tally is the hand-kept number K-863 and K-1379 both record going stale: the rake file is cut at every `task` declaration and each task must carry exactly one block if it boots a server and none if it does not, which is derived from the file and needs no edit here when a demo gains a task. Two tasks failed it the day it was written and were fixed rather than excepted — getgrocery's `demo:pow` and hoteling's `demo:browse` both spawned a server and hard-coded `http://127.0.0.1:#{port}`, so K-1400's own prose («every task that boots one opens with the same block») had been false since it was written and nothing could have said so. Those two runs now print the /etc/hosts hint once and follow `KIOSK_DEMO_HOST_LOOKUP=1` to the vanity name like their siblings; with the variable unset, which is every CI run and every fresh checkout, nothing about them changes. Intent: make the guard hold the property its own comment claims, in a form that cannot go stale when the population moves again.

- 2026-09-08: **the engine drew every operator's verbs from one wildcard pair, and no operator could read their own wire off their own routes file (T-183, K-1394).** Phil, on finding that the published onboarding guide sells `mount Kiosk::Server::Engine` as «one line… you write no route of your own» while not one of the nine shipped origins mounted anything: «Я НЕ СОГЛАСЕН с тем что у нас должна быть какая-то магия с роутами. Сделаем пока по-простому. GET/QUERY для query, POST для run. Вручную для каждого в routes. Во всех демках route'ы - в ОТДЕЛЬНОМ файле, типа config/routes/kiosk.rb», amended the same day to «ОБЩИЕ РОУТЫ… ВОТ НУЖНО маунтить автоматом. Но так, как обычно Rails engine маунтится.» So the split is by WHOSE surface it is, and both halves moved at once. **The protocol plane is MOUNTED** — `schema`, `pay`, the JWKS and OpenAPI documents, the four `auth/*` ceremonies, the RFC 8628 device-grant pair, the link/claim/unlink endpoints, the «Link an assistant» pages, the KYC attestation endpoint and the root-relative discovery surface — because those paths are the spec's and not the operator's, and every one of the seven operator demos plus the e2e origin now draws them with one `mount` line instead of the thirty-odd hand-copied lines each carried. **The operator's own verbs are HAND-WRITTEN**, one explicit route each, with the METHOD FOLLOWING THE KIND: GET for a `kind :query`, POST for a `kind :action`, the name pinned with `defaults: { kiosk_verb: … }`. Fifty-two routes across eight origins, all of them in a file of their own — `config/routes/kiosk.rb`, reached by Rails' own `draw(:kiosk)` — so `bin/rails routes` finally prints an origin's actual wire and a demo's own pages stop being interleaved with the protocol. **The `get`/`post "/kiosk/:kiosk_verb"` pair is deleted from the engine and from all nine files.** That pair bought one real thing and losing it is the price of the change: «declared but unrouted / routed but undeclared» could not occur while a wildcard resolved names at request time, and it can occur now. `bin/check-verb-routes` is the replacement — it DERIVES each origin's expected list from the handler controllers that origin's own initializer names in `c.handlers`, types no verb name anywhere, and fails on a verb with no route, a route with no verb, a method that disagrees with the kind, a path that disagrees with its pinned name, a dynamic segment, a collision with a path the engine draws, a verb line above the mount, or a `config/routes.rb` that never calls `draw(:kiosk)`. Twelve planted breaks in its own `--self-test`, its own CI job. **The ordering property had to be re-established deliberately, because it changed shape.** It used to be internal to one table: the engine drew its reserved lines first and its catch-all last, so an operator verb called `schema` was shadowed by construction. It is now a property of a file somebody writes — the mount is the FIRST line and the verbs come after it — so it is held in three places instead of assumed in one: `HandlerMixin::RESERVED_NAMES` still raises at class-body load so the verb cannot be DECLARED, the new guard's NOT-RESERVED and MOUNT-FIRST rules refuse the route at build time, and `engine_mount_spec.rb` now measures the routing layer's own answer on a booted host with a shadow route deliberately planted below the mount. **One thing the deletion would have broken silently, so it did not:** an unregistered name has no explicit route by construction, and spec §8.1/§9 make `404 verb_not_found` and `405` + `Allow` MANDATORY of an operator. The engine therefore appends a REFUSAL route below the host's own routes — `VerbRefusalController`, which resolves identity, answers those two statuses, and RAISES rather than serving when the name IS registered as the kind asked for, because serving there would be the deleted wildcard with an extra step. `GET /kiosk/nope/nope` stays a Rails routing 404, so the K-824 header probe still probes what it was written for. **Two demos gained a path they used to lack, and their prose was corrected rather than the mount narrowed:** philslist and tudu now draw `POST /kiosk/pay`, because the mount draws the whole protocol plane, and it answers the wire's own 403 — neither configures a `payment_provider`, so `pay` is still absent from `capabilities`, from `agents.json` and from `agents.txt`, which is where the «no payments here» claim was always demonstrable. Five demos gained `POST /kiosk/agents/kyc` and three gained the assistants pages on the same terms. Intent: an operator can read their wire off their routes file, the protocol keeps the half that is not theirs to write, and the bug class that trade re-opens is held by a gate rather than by care.

- 2026-09-08: **getgrocery completed a partial delivery date from the SERVER PROCESS's clock, on an origin whose own file says «one clock for the whole origin» three times (K-1411).** `Date.parse` accepts values naming only part of a date — «Tue», «sep», «1st», «250» — and fills the rest in from `Date.today`, which reads whatever zone the box happens to be in. Everything else on this surface reads `DeliverySlots.now` in Europe/Dublin, so a server a day ahead of or behind Dublin resolved «1st» on the thirtieth to a different MONTH and «Tue» across a week boundary to a different WEEK: a wrong delivery date rather than a refusal, which is the direction that hurts. Both readers now go through `WireArguments.parse_date`, which hands a value carrying its own year straight to `Date.parse` — an answer no clock can affect — and completes anything else from the delivery zone's today, reproducing `Date.parse`'s own filling rules for a lone weekday, month, day-of-month, ordinal day and ISO week. WHAT FORMS ARE ACCEPTED IS UNCHANGED and deliberately so, because that question is open and is not this change's to answer: the spec asserts form by form that with the origin's today set to the runner's today the two agree on all 45 spellings it names, including the ones neither accepts. The demo's own record was the other half of the defect — it asserted three loose forms and the parser took far more — so the spec now states what it really does, the ambiguous day-first `09/01/2026` (nine January, not one September) among them. The published onboarding guide teaches these verbs and carries the same completion, because it already warned against `Date.today` in the paragraph above the one that used it.

- 2026-09-08: **getgrocery's back office showed a Dublin delivery window beside a UTC «Ordered» time, with nothing on the card saying which was which (K-1414).** The K-1391 wave moved the window onto `DeliverySlots.zone` and left the order timestamp on the connection's clock, so fixing one of the two produced a single screen carrying two clocks — arguably worse for the shop's own staff than the uniform wrongness it replaced. `created_at` is a `timestamptz` and arrives on the server's clock for exactly the reason `slot_at` does, so it is now composed in the controller through the same zone and NAMES it, the way `DeliverySlots.label` already names it for the window. Intent: one screen, one clock, both of them said out loud.

- 2026-09-08: **hoteling's `search_hotels` descriptor restated shape its own schemas declare, and restated one response header wrongly (K-1407).** ADR-0023 scopes `description` to semantics and the published descriptor house style names the prohibitions in terms: no required/optional marker, no unit, no currency, no default. Three clauses were none of prose's business — «Every filter is optional», which `required: []` two declarations down already states; «Prices are EUR cents», a UNIT that `max_price_cents` and `from_price_cents` each carry on their own field; and «carts are signed in eur», which the row's `currency` field carries. The fourth was worse than redundant: the descriptor called `X-Total-Count` «how you tell a short page from the end of the results», where the specification and the skill both say the loop bound is the absence of the `rel="next"` link and that this header is advisory — so the operator's prose contradicted the protocol on the one point an assistant acts on. The page-size default and its clamp STAY, and the file now records why in a form a later reader can check: measured at head, the engine's derived OpenAPI injects `limit` as `{type: "integer", minimum: 1}` with no `default:` and no `maximum:`, so twenty and fifty are stated nowhere else on this verb, which is precisely the carve-out the house style grants. The same two unit restatements were removed from `availability` and `hotel_detail` in the same file, and `hotel_detail` lost an «ARE OPTIONAL» marker while keeping the sentence that says what the dates CHANGE — which is the half no schema can hold.

- 2026-09-08: **an archived row's reason for rejecting a sibling checkout named a cost that does not exist, and the next wave to weigh the option would have inherited it (K-1385).** K-1360 recorded that giving `check-onboarding-derivation`'s CI leg a sibling checkout «would need a cross-repo token, for a repository that is private today». Measured with `gh api`, both `kiosk-hq/kiosk.tech` and `kiosk-hq/kiosk` answer `private: false, visibility: public`, and the `published-bytes` job in this repo's own workflow already checks that sibling out with no token at all. The archive is frozen history and is not rewritten; what changed is the place a future wave actually reads — the guard's own `--self-test` header — which now states the real reason the fixture stays: a leg that can only be proven where the sibling happens to be present reads green in every hand-run and is red on the push, which is exactly what happened to this job once. Intent: keep the decision, replace the false reason for it with the true one.

- 2026-09-08: **five public documents still narrated elements a reader can no longer meet, which the constitution's new rule forbids on sight (K-1409).** Phil's rule of 2026-09-07 — «Введи правило это убирать сразу. Этому не место в публичных артефактах кроме очень кратко changelog'а» — is about ORDER as much as presence: a reader arriving today has no memory to correct, and a paragraph that opens with what something USED to be spends their attention before it tells them what it is. Cleared: getgrocery's README on a field's former name, atablefor's README on a spelling `my_bookings` no longer answers, four passages in stylish's README (a summary sentence that had been wrong, a retired parse, two deleted IdP stand-ins), the equihash README on what a rejected proof used to cost, and one sentence on the published `not_found` problem page about two codes that were once one. In every case the LIVE half is kept and re-stated in the present tense, because the rule does not license deleting a caveat that still binds: the TZ hazard stylish's clock spec exists for is a hazard today, and the cost gap the equihash verifier buys is a live argument. What was judged live and KEPT is the useful half of the sweep and is recorded on the ledger row rather than here.

- 2026-09-07: **the normative PoW schema typed one algorithm's nonce as every algorithm's nonce, and turning on request validation refused the rest before the gate saw them (K-1410).** ADR-0001 decision 3 says two things that had drifted apart. On PACKAGING it is settled and narrow: equihash is the default and only shipped backend, `kiosk-pow` (Argon2id) is retired by name, and the recorded reason is that verifying an Argon2id proof costs one m-sized evaluation, so a bad-proof flood is a DoS on the verifier. On the WIRE the same decision says the opposite, deliberately: the challenge carries `{alg, params}` and is «algorithm-agnostic on the wire», because an operator may bring a backend this project does not define. `pow.schema.json` implemented only the first half — it typed a proof's `nonce` as the Equihash solution object outright — so with `validate_requests` on (the K-1399 default) every valid non-equihash proof was a 400 before any verifier could refuse it on its own terms. The schema now types `nonce` CONDITIONALLY on `challenge.alg`: the Equihash object when and only when the challenge says equihash, and unconstrained otherwise, leaving the encoding to the backend that named itself. Both normative documents moved with it — `specification.html` and `spec/protocol.md` now say that the nonce's shape belongs to the algorithm, that an operator refuses an ALGORITHM rather than a nonce shape they have no definition for — and the vendored copy in `kiosk-server` follows, held byte-for-byte by `bin/check-spec-schemas`. The 400's own hint stopped naming `{indices}` flatly, for the same reason. The seven engine examples that exercise the retired Argon2id backend over the ADR-0022 header transport had turned the shape check OFF and recorded «the schema is Equihash-only» as the reason; the flag is back at its default and the file is green with validation ON, which is now the regression test for exactly this. Intent: the formal spec states what the recorded decision decided, and the one artefact that could refuse a conforming implementation stops doing it.

- 2026-09-07: **the pagination cursor bought opacity it did not have, and a mangled one silently answered page one (K-1403).** `Cursor.encode_offset` wrapped `offset:N` in urlsafe base64 and `decode_offset` returned the default for anything it could not read. The encoding was a costume — not a secret, not signed, not stable across a collection changing under the reader, decodable by anyone in one line — and it told a reader «opaque, do not parse» while providing none of the properties opacity is for, which is worse than saying nothing: a reader who believes the token is protected reasons about it wrongly. The lenience was the same defect on the other side, answering an assistant that had corrupted its cursor with plausible rows and no signal. The helper now publishes the offset as a decimal integer and says so in its own documentation; an ABSENT cursor is still the first page, and anything present that is not a non-negative decimal integer is a typed 400 naming the parameter and steering to the `Link` target. Nothing is signed, and the reason is stated rather than assumed: a cursor authorizes nothing, so a forged `cursor=5000` reaches exactly the rows the same request could have reached by following five thousand links, and an HMAC would hide a handler with no scoping rather than fix it. The two normative documents gained the clause that settles it for adopters — opacity is a CONTRACT on the assistant, not a property of the token; a cursor MAY be clear text; it MUST NOT be treated as an authorization token; and an operator refusing one it did not issue SHOULD answer `400 bad_request` rather than serve page one. hoteling's `search_hotels` lost the negative-offset clamp with the lenience that made it necessary. Intent: the token says what it is, and a wrong one is answered rather than absorbed.

- 2026-09-07: **the PSP decorator on the money path forwarded everything it did not know (K-1402).** `ValidatingBookingProvider` — hoteling's cashier check, the thing that verifies an agent-signed cart against the operator's own quote before a capture — carried `method_missing` and `respond_to_missing?` straight through to the wrapped provider. Its surface was therefore «whatever the wrapped object happens to answer»: a method added to a PSP adapter upstream became silently reachable THROUGH the validating wrapper without passing any of its checks, and a typo at a call site became a delegation instead of a `NoMethodError`. Neither shows up in a diff of that file. The port is now enumerated — `capture` (overridden, the cashier), `setup_required?` and `setup_url` (forwarded, the two an operator's app actually calls) — with `setup_url` documented as the optional half that `PaymentProviders::Base` does not define and that the Stripe swap needs. Everything else on the wrapped object is unreachable through the wrapper by construction. Intent: on the path that moves money, adding a method is a decision somebody makes in that file with the checks in front of them.

- 2026-09-07: **the operator back office read the delivery window on the server's clock (K-1391).** getgrocery's admin orders page plucked `slot_at` raw and re-parsed it in the view, so a `timestamptz` that came back on whatever clock the connection was using rendered «07:00» where the customer's assistant had been told «08:00–10:00 (Europe/Dublin)». The wire had been made self-consistent by the K-1370 wave and the shop's own staff screen had not, which is the more embarrassing half. The controller now renders the window through `DeliverySlots` — the one writer `delivery_slots` and `my_orders` already answer from — and the view prints it. Intent: the time of the service, at the place the service happens, in one voice for staff and assistant alike.

- 2026-09-07: **three operator demos booted servers on vanity hosts nobody was told existed, and the reason was a correlation with documents (K-1400, K-1379).** philslist, stylish and tudu have deployed `<demo>.demo.kiosk.tech` names in `deploy/README.md` and hard-coded `http://127.0.0.1:#{port}` in every task, printing no hint that a vanity name was reachable — while four other demos shipped an opt-in host-detection block in every task that boots. K-1379 measured the cause and found no decision behind it: the four with the block are EXACTLY the four with a published `before-after.md`, a correlation with the documents rather than with any capability, and not chronological either. Nothing made the block inapplicable, so it is now in all seven, sixteen further sites, and `bin/check-demo-copies` holds all seven in lockstep. WHAT AN OPERATOR'S RUN PRINTS CHANGED, which is the whole visible effect: a task in those three now prints once «(using 127.0.0.1 — to reach `<demo>.demo.kiosk.tech` instead, add it to /etc/hosts as 127.0.0.1 and set KIOSK_DEMO_HOST_LOOKUP=1)», and with that variable AND an /etc/hosts entry it dials the vanity name. The lookup stays opt-in, so no run gains an outbound DNS query it did not have; the server is still bound to 127.0.0.1 everywhere; only the URL the harness dials follows the name. The three apps gained the `config.hosts <<` entry the block's own comment requires, without which the printed /etc/hosts line would earn a Rails 8 HostAuthorization 403. `kiosk-demo-prove` is deliberately out: it is an issuer, not a Kiosk operator, and boots no server of its own. Intent: what a demo tells its operator is a decision, not a side effect of which demos happened to get a narrative document.

- 2026-09-07: **a second unconditional `db:drop` hid inside the first one's warning, and a third dropped a different database entirely (K-1393).** `bin/check-demo-prereqs` asked one question of a demo's whole rake file — «does anything here run a `db:drop db:create`» — and rendered one sentence about `demo:setup` «and every task that depends on it». getgrocery's `demo:rls` runs its own drop and depends on nothing, so an operator who avoided `demo:setup` on the strength of that warning still lost the database; kiosk-demo-prove's `demo:test` runs the same command under `RAILS_ENV=test`, which the warning described with complete confidence as the development database. Ownership is now DERIVED task by task from the same file, with the `RAILS_ENV=` that prefixes the command, and the environment is a parameter of the `config/database.yml` read rather than hard-coded to `development:`. No task name is typed anywhere. Two vacuity arms fail if the walk stops attributing anything or if no second drop exists anywhere, and three new planted breaks cover the deleted sentence, a new task that grows a drop, and a renamed environment prefix — seventeen arms in that self-test now. **The three `desc` strings stay silent about the sibling database, and that is recorded rather than assumed (K-1392):** measured on rake 13.4.2, `rake -T` renders only a description's FIRST SENTENCE, so «derive the desc» does not mean adding a sentence — it means putting the warning in FRONT of what the task is, and the `-T` line then stops saying what the task does. The two surfaces that do carry it carry it better: the generated README block names the sibling demo and its database from a derivation no hand-copy can rot, and `script/prove_broker_boot.rb` prints the drop at the moment it happens. Intent: every destructive command is attributed to the task that owns it and to the database it actually destroys.

- 2026-09-07: **the one `bin/check-*` whose satisfied state was a non-zero exit (K-1412).** `bin/check-conformance-drift` run bare printed its triage list — the anchors whose cited region has moved since the last sweep — and exited 2. The distinction from exit 1 («cannot answer») was deliberate and the consequence was not: every other `bin/check-*` in that directory exits 0 when satisfied, so a session sweeping the directory in a loop read this one member as RED on the state its own output calls «a TRIAGE LIST, not a failure». That is either a false failure reported to a human or, worse, a reader who learns to ignore a non-zero exit from that directory — and a tool whose normal state is non-zero teaches people to stop reading the one signal that cannot be faked. The bare run now exits 0 and prints the same list with the same count; `--exit-on-drift` gives 2 to a caller that explicitly wants a status to branch on. Exit 1 still means the question could not be answered, in both modes. Renaming it out of the `check-*` namespace was the alternative and was rejected: the name is cited by CI, by `audit/SWEEP.md` and by frozen ledger and report entries that must not be rewritten. Intent: an exit code means the same thing across a directory, or it means nothing.

- 2026-09-07: **the reputation seam had two words for one thing and the wrong one failed silently; it is now a load error (K-1395).** An operator DECLARES a handler `kind :query` or `kind :action`, but the PoW/reputation hooks are asked about a call in the gate's own coarse vocabulary — `Executor::VERBS`, `%i[query run pay]` — in which an `action` arrives as `:run`. `query` is the one word spelled identically in both, which is exactly what hides the difference: a policy written `verb == :action` matches nothing, returns nil, and nil is the ordinary «do not toll this one» answer. No error, no log line, no failing test, and the toll never applies to a single write — on the surface that prices abuse. K-1329 had already made the mapping normative (protocol §13 and the matching paragraph of `specification.html` both state that the write kind is named `run`), so which word wins was settled by the authority chain and not re-litigated here: the spec publishes `run`, the code keeps `run`, and what changed is that the OTHER spelling stopped being silent. `Kiosk.configuration.reputation_policy=` and `reputation_factors=` now parse the hook's own source (Prism) and raise `Errors::ConfigurationError` at boot, naming `:run`, when it compares the verb against `:action` — as `==`/`!=`, as a `when` clause, or through an array `include?`. Deliberately NOT an alias: handing the hook a value that answers `== :action` as well as `== :run` would fix the `==` spelling and break the `case` spelling in the same stroke, because `case verb when :run` dispatches through `Symbol#===` whose receiver is the literal. Deliberately NOT a grep either — a bare `:action` in a hash key, a string or a comment is untouched, since a false load error on a correct policy would be worse than the thing being prevented. What it cannot read (an object that does not answer the hook, an unreadable source file, a Ruby without Prism) returns `:unreadable` and raises nothing, and the arm asserts a well-formed policy comes back `:clean`, so a parser that silently stopped reading turns an example red instead of turning the guard into a no-op. Intent: one vocabulary, and the other spelling loud at boot rather than inert forever.

- 2026-09-07: **«the operator's Ruby server never executes a subprocess to verify a proof» was true and guarded by nothing; now it is guarded (K-1396).** Phil's constraint, verbatim: «Ruby Kiosk сервер, крутящий код оператора, не должен исполнять python для проверки PoW». Measured before writing anything: the equihash gem's `lib/` contains no `Open3`, `system`, backticks, `IO.popen`, `spawn` or `exec` outside comments, `solver_path` is called by nothing a served request loads, and verify is pure Ruby. The defect was that a shell-out added to that path tomorrow would redden no suite — `verify` would still return true and every test would still pass, while the origin quietly acquired a subprocess reachable from an unauthenticated 402 retry. `bin/check-served-pow-purity` now derives the SERVED set rather than listing it: the transitive `require` closure of the engine, the reputation layer, and whatever PoW backend an operator initializer requires (that entry is read out of the demo initializers, so a demo adding a backend widens the guard by itself), resolved against this repo's `*/lib` roots. It fails on any process-spawning construct in that closure, and on a served file that starts naming the solver. It is deliberately NOT a whole-repo grep: the client scripts legitimately run `python3 solve.py`, and a guard that failed on them would be switched off in a week — so the scope is stated in the script's own output alongside the closure size. Detection is AST-level because the equihash gem's documentation shows `Open3.capture2("python3", …)` in a comment to a CALLER, and a textual guard would either fail on that comment forever or be weakened until it failed on nothing; the self-test asserts that file is IN the closure, contains the text `Open3`, and scans clean anyway. A new CI job runs both arms. The gem README now states the split in one sentence: verifying is the operator's Ruby, solving is the caller's problem. Intent: a security property nobody can regress by accident is worth more than the same property held by whoever last remembered it.

- 2026-09-07: **`validate_requests` defaulted to the setting whose failure is silent, and every shipped origin turned it the other way (K-1399).** The flag makes the wire reject a malformed `Kiosk-PoW` with a typed 400 naming the shape it wanted; with it off, a proof the gate cannot parse yields no proofs at all, so the gate re-issues a fresh 402 forever with no diagnostic — the K-479 loop, in which the assistant on the other end can neither see nor fix what it sent. It shipped `false` as «byte-identical old behaviour», and then all seven showcase origins set it true and `rails g kiosk:install` started writing it true (K-1336): a default nobody wanted, and one an adopter who skipped the generator inherited in silence. It is now `true`. The sibling flag `validate_responses` stays `false` and the asymmetry is the content of the decision rather than an inconsistency: a request-shape refusal is a 400 to a caller who sent a bad request; a response-shape refusal is a 500 to a caller who did nothing wrong, so the reason the one is off in production does not transfer to the other. The reader had to stop being `@x ||= false` in the same change — with a true default that idiom turns an operator's explicit `false` back into `true`, and an opt-out that silently does nothing is the same class of silence the flip exists to remove; an example asserts the opt-out sticks. This is a behaviour change for an existing adopter and the honest accounting is that there are none, because nothing is published to RubyGems — written down rather than assumed, since that will stop being true. One spec file turns the flag off explicitly now and says why: it exercises the ADR-0022 header transport against the OPTIONAL argon2id backend, whose solution is a decimal string, and the vendored normative PoW schema types a proof's `nonce` as an Equihash solution object — so with the shape check on, an argon2id proof is refused before the transport under test is reached. Equihash is the one shipped backend and every demo uses it, so nothing an operator serves is affected. Intent: the default is the configuration the project actually recommends, and the one whose failure mode is loud.

- 2026-09-07: **a dependency line that changed nothing in seven files, and two READMEs that taught a protocol that no longer exists (K-1397, K-1398).** `json_schemer` was declared in all seven demo Gemfiles with a comment explaining that it is a runtime dependency of `kiosk-server` and would be installed transitively anyway — which is precisely why the line should not have been there, in the file an adopting operator copies first. It is gone from all seven; the resolution is unchanged, and each lockfile lost exactly one line, its top-level `DEPENDENCIES` entry, while the gem stays resolved under `kiosk-server` as it always was. Four published `before-after.md` narratives quoted that Gemfile and explained the line, so `bin/check-demo-derivations` went red the moment the line left — which is the guard doing its job: the quoted block, its `abridged:` declaration and the paragraph beneath it all moved with the file, and the paragraph now says the true thing (`json_schemer` needs no line at all) rather than the thing that was true while the line existed. Separately, skooti's and tudu's READMEs each explained that the 0.3 `POST /kiosk/query` and `POST /kiosk/run` «are the ordinary 404 an authenticated caller gets» — a new reader told about endpoints that are gone before being told about the ones that are there. Phil's rule, filed the same day into the workspace constitution: «Введи правило это убирать сразу. Этому не место в публичных артефактах кроме очень кратко changelog'а.» Both passages are gone. What was kept in both places is the LIVE half of each sentence, which is a different thing from its history: a path naming no registered verb really is a 404 to an authenticated caller and a 401 without a bearer, because the wire resolves the caller before it looks the verb up, and that is a fact a reader can still meet. tudu's red-team battery still names its `RetiredWire` beat, because the beat exists and prose that omitted it would be a second inaccuracy; what it no longer does is narrate the wire the beat probes. Intent: a public artefact describes what is, and the afterlife of a removed element is one changelog line — this one.

- 2026-09-07: **five guards were trusted on prose rather than on a mechanism, and one flag answered a different question than the one it was given (K-1368, K-1369, K-1379, K-1380, K-1383, K-1388, K-1389).** Nothing here changes what the demos or the gems do; all of it changes what this repository can still be wrong about without saying so. **`bin/check-gem-packaging` had no `--self-test` at all**, so its newest rule — a packaged file that teaches an install must point at the README section stating publication status — was held by a paragraph and by whoever remembered to plant a break by hand. It now runs ten arms over a MINIATURE workspace: two tiny gems, a git index, and this script copied into the fixture's own `bin/`, because `ROOT` comes off `__dir__`. Seven are planted breaks (a gemspec that forgets `app/`, exactly the omission this file was written for; a `__dir__` path that escapes the package; a shape the resolver cannot read, which must ERROR rather than skip; a packaged README that points nowhere; publication day removing the `github:` qualifier the pointer is derived from) and three are vacuity arms that remove a rule's SUBJECT rather than violate it, because for a presence requirement «nothing matched» is the rule not running. 3.2 s, and the runner that executes every guard declaring the flag now enrols it. **`bin/check-demo-copies` gained the rule that stops K-1323 regrowing**: a ledger id OUTSIDE a comment, anywhere in the Ruby a demo runs, is now a build failure — a `K-1099` in a `desc` is printed at a terminal to an operator who cannot open the row it names, and the same id in a comment is a note to the next maintainer and is left entirely alone, which is the line Phil's instruction actually draws. Comment-versus-code is asked of Ripper rather than of a regex, because `^\s*#` misses a trailing comment and a `#` in a heredoc is not one. Measured before writing it: 24 occurrences under `kiosk-demo-*`, 19 of them in the Ruby this scans, and **every one of those in a comment** — so the ban starts at zero survivors and needs no exemption list. It also closed the one bannered invariant whose own `--self-test` note said it had NO arm (the same ban one directory over, in the install-generator templates), so the coverage table is now 58 arms over 27 of 27 invariants; and `HOST_BLOCK_SHIPS` — the only `ships:` set in that file with no reason — got one, recording what a measurement rather than a memory says: the three operator demos left out boot servers and have deployed vanity hosts, nothing makes the block inapplicable to them, no ADR or ledger row records a choice, and the four listed are exactly the four with a published `before-after.md`. That is a partial sweep, and the declaration now says so instead of reading as a decision. **`bin/check-onboarding-derivation --network` was silently overridden by `KIOSK_TECH_DIR`**: the variable was resolved first, so a run asked for the published page and was handed a local checkout, with a clean exit code and a run line naming the checkout. The flag now outranks the variable, an override says so in the provenance line, `--page` together with `--network` is refused rather than silently resolved, and two arms hold it — one pointing the variable at a decoy page that must never come back. Its self-test also stopped deriving its own planted-versus-vacuity split by subtracting two totals: each arm declares its kind, the summary counts, and a declared kind that runs zero arms fails. **`deploy/production-smoke.sh` now re-measures the fact its own posture rests on** — that exactly one request in it carries an `Authorization:` header — so the next `curl` added there cannot widen the unvalidated surface while the justification above still reads as measured. Intent: a rule proved by prose is a rule nobody can re-prove, and a flag that quietly answers a different question is worse than one that fails.

- 2026-09-07: **`bin/check-onboarding-derivation --network` had never once evaluated its last three rules, because it crashed on every run there has ever been (K-1367).** Run against the published page it raised `Encoding::CompatibilityError: incompatible character encodings: BINARY (ASCII-8BIT) and UTF-8`, unrescued, out of `run_rules` at rule 13 of 15 — so ERROR_REDACTION, PROPERTY_KEYWORDS and TOTAL_GUARD, the three rules added most recently, had never been asked about the surface an operator is actually reading, and no run summary was printed. The body is now decoded once, at the seam that produces it: the response's declared charset when it names one, UTF-8 otherwise, `scrub` for bytes that are neither — a page served with a broken byte is a fact about the page and must reach the rules as text.

  **The 37-arm `--self-test` could not see it, and that is the more useful half of this entry.** Every arm was FILE-BACKED, so every arm ran over a UTF-8 string and none of them ever touched the network reader; the mode with the crash was the one mode nothing exercised. Two arms now drive `resolve_page` through the real `--network` branch with the response `Net::HTTP` actually returns — no socket is opened, `get_response` is aliased away for the duration and put back — and they assert both halves: that an ASCII-8BIT body reaches every rule as text, and that a break planted in those BYTES still goes red. The second is what stops the repair degenerating into «return something that never raises»; measured, a `decoded_body` that returns an empty string satisfies nothing-raised and is caught by the first arm. Watched red on the pre-fix seam restored into the file, then restored from a copy with the sha re-checked. Intent: a mode no arm exercises is a mode nobody is checking, whatever the arm count says.

- 2026-09-07: **`e2e/run.sh` and kiosk-server's suite went red on a tree nobody had touched — json 3.0.0 was published this morning and the newest released activesupport cannot read it (K-1384).** json 3.0.0 reached RubyGems at 06:57 UTC and removed the POSITIONAL options argument from `JSON.parse`; the signature is now `parse(source, on_load:, object_class:, array_class:, **options)`, so `JSON.parse(str, {})` raises `ArgumentError (wrong number of arguments (given 2, expected 1))`. `activesupport 8.1.3.1` — the NEWEST release, and the only line `kiosk-server.gemspec` allows (`~> 8.1`) — still spells it positionally in `ActiveSupport::JSON.decode`, and declares `json (>= 0)` with no upper bound at all. Two ordinary Rails paths reach that call, and the backtrace was read out of the generated app rather than inferred: `ActionDispatch::Cookies::EncryptedKeyRotatingCookieJar#parse` → `MessageEncryptor#read_message` → `Metadata#deserialize_from_json`, so the session cookie cannot be decrypted and **every Devise sign-in answers 500** — the harness dies at the binding ceremony with `DeviseSession::SignInError: sign-in failed: 500`; and `ActionDispatch::Http::Parameters`' default JSON parser, so a wire POST with a JSON body is refused 400 instead of executed. Neither is Kiosk code, and nothing in this repository pinned `json`, so the break arrived from the registry rather than from a commit.

  **The blast radius, measured rather than reasoned.** The eight committed demo lockfiles resolve json 2.19.9–2.21.2 beside activesupport, so the demos are green today and go red on the next `bundle update` in any of them. Three bundles here have NO committed lockfile and resolve activesupport, which means CI resolves them fresh and takes whatever the registry has: kiosk-server (**1609 examples, 19 failures** under json 3.0.0, against 0 failures under 2.19.8), kiosk-all and kiosk-rls (both green under json 3.0.0 — but only because their suites never reach a Rails request, which is green by accident and not by construction). And an operator following the published guide hits this before Kiosk is involved at all: a fresh `rails ~> 8.1` + `devise` Gemfile resolves rails 8.1.3.1, devise 5.0.4 and **json 3.0.0**, so the app is broken on the day it is generated. That half is upstream and not ours to repair — `rails/rails` main already spells it `::JSON.parse(json, **options)`, and no RELEASE carries that yet.

  **What changed: `gem "json", "< 3.0"` in the four bundles that resolve fresh** — the Gemfile `e2e/run.sh` generates, plus kiosk-server's, kiosk-all's and kiosk-rls's. Raising the activesupport floor was measured and is not an option: 8.1.3.1 IS the newest published version. The demos are deliberately NOT pinned; their committed lockfiles already answer the question, and a constraint in a Gemfile whose lock is committed would hide the event that actually matters (someone running `bundle update`) behind a line nobody would revisit.

  **The pin retires itself, because a removal condition that lives only in a comment is a condition nobody checks.** `bin/check-json-pin` (its own CI job) reads `::JSON.parse(json, options)` out of the activesupport that is actually resolved and goes RED the day a release carries the keyword-splat form — which is the day the pin must come off, whoever is on shift. It also holds every `gem "json"` declaration in the tracked tree to one spelling with a comment naming the row and stating a removal condition, and every committed `Gemfile.lock` that resolves activesupport to a json below 3.0, which is how the eight demos are covered without being pinned. Watched red on a planted json 3.0.0 in `kiosk-demo-tudu/Gemfile.lock` and restored from a file copy; thirteen fixture arms plus two live vacuity arms in `--self-test`. Intent: an incompatibility that arrives from the registry should break loudly once, be pinned only where something actually resolves, and un-pin itself rather than waiting for someone to remember why it was there.

Measured in a CI-faithful two-repository layout, with `kiosk.tech` at `origin/main` rather than at the local branch: all three steps exit 0, `12 examples, 0 failures` and nothing pending, and all three red on the planted half-relink.

  **What this buys and what it costs.** A relink wave now spans two repositories in a fixed order — push `kiosk.tech` FIRST — because the pinned cut must be on the sibling's default branch before this repo points at it. That is the assistant-visible truth rather than an artefact of the check: in the other order the pins name a file the world cannot fetch. The sibling checkout is legitimate here and nowhere else in this workflow because both repositories are public (no cross-repo secret) and a published `skill-vX.Y.Z.md` is IMMUTABLE by rule, so the comparison is against a frozen artefact rather than a moving branch — which is what separates it from `solver-pin-live`, whose subject is a DEPLOY and which therefore runs on a schedule. Intent: the one instruction an assistant is given about how to speak this wire should be held by something other than the memory of whoever last had two checkouts side by side.

- 2026-09-07: **the CI job that was supposed to push-block on the onboarding guard could only ever have been RED, and it read green everywhere anybody ran it (K-1360).** `.github/workflows/ci.yml`'s `onboarding-derivation` job runs `./bin/check-onboarding-derivation --self-test` under a step named «no network, no sibling», and that is exactly the condition it failed in. Nothing had been pushed, so CI had never executed the job; the first push would have gone red, and that push is the publication path.

  **The fixture was the defect, not the arms.** The guard's own header has always claimed the self-test «runs with no sibling and no network, which is the whole reason it can be the push-blocking half of the CI job», and that the fixture it plants into is «a minimal page that satisfies every rule by construction». It was not: it wrote every property as a bare `{ type: "string" }`, so a page built from it OMITTED the `format`, `minimum`, `maximum`, `minItems` and `pattern` that getgrocery declares — R14 is red on such a page — and it declared no array ELEMENT schema at all, which is the depth K-1354 taught that rule to read. A constant that resolves to nothing aborts the fixture build by name rather than quietly dropping the keyword, because a dropped keyword would hand R14 two copies of a hole instead of a comparison. The alternative remedy — letting the fixture mode SKIP its page-dependent arms — was refused: a leg that passes by covering less is the failure this guard exists to detect, and it converts a loud red into a quiet false green.

  **Two smaller things fell out of building it and both were live holes.** `assertion_value` reads a non-bracketed value to the comma OR THE END OF THE LINE, so a keyword written last on its line comes back with the closing brace attached — `"date" }` against getgrocery's `"date"` — which the origin never hits because its declarations are multi-line; every keyword the fixture writes therefore ends in a comma. And the fixture carried neither half of R13's redaction split, so ERROR_REDACTION was red on the unplanted fixture and its two arms were observing a rule that was already failing — coverage in the log and nothing behind it. No workflow file changed: the job's step name is now true. Intent: a push-blocking leg has to be proved in the environment that runs it, and «it passed on my machine» is a different question from «it passes on the runner» whenever the two have different files in them.

- 2026-09-07: **the onboarding guide's copied cart bounded each LINE and never the cart, so a request its own published schema calls valid still answered `500` — and nothing here could have seen it (K-1359).** The wave before this one gave the page `qty`'s declared `maximum`, the width of the `order_items.qty` column, and a derivation rule holds it there. MEASURED at head against the demo's own bundle: serialising the column's own maximum through `ActiveModel::Type::Integer.new(limit: 4)` returns fine, and serialising the total an in-range quantity of the catalogue's cheapest row produces raises `ActiveModel::RangeError` — in Ruby, before any SQL, which the executor's `rescue StandardError` serves as `500 action_failed` for an argument a client simply got wrong. That is the shape K-1047 recorded on getgrocery itself and closed there with `WireArguments.priceable_total`; the guide was still carrying the pre-fix half of it.

  **The extension is a NEW rule rather than an arm of `PROPERTY_KEYWORDS`, and the reason is the SUBJECT rather than the implementation.** That rule walks each side's `properties:` hashes and compares the JSON Schema assertion keywords declared there. A cart total is not a property of anything — it is a value the handler COMPUTES from the operator's own catalogue prices, and no per-property keyword can express a bound on a sum. That is not an accident of how the rule is written: it is the same reason getgrocery's own guard is a handler refusal and not a `maximum`, stated in `wire_arguments.rb` and in `create_order`'s published description. Extending it would have given a single rule a second, unrelated subject with a second, unrelated derivation, and its printed line about shared properties would then have been true of a run that also silently asserted something about a handler body. So `TOTAL_GUARD` is its own rule, in the family of the bans that already read handler bodies, and it DERIVES its ceiling by walking the origin rather than naming it: the local the create operation binds its summed total to, the module function that local is handed to, the constant that function bounds it against, and the literal that constant is assigned. A link that does not resolve is reported as the reason the rule holds nothing, never passed over — the state K-1354 closed on the neighbouring rule.

  Per summing handler on the page it asserts that the sum is BOUND to a local — a sum written straight into the call that writes it is unrefusable by construction, and that was the defect — that the local is compared against the DERIVED ceiling, since a different number is an invented basket size where the bound is the column's, and that the comparison is the FIRST use of the local, because a guard the value has already been consumed past is not a guard and the origin takes its refusal the moment prices resolve and before the replace path locks a row. Every one was planted and watched red, alongside a vacuity arm that breaks the first link of the derivation chain; the built-in fixture grew a summing verb so the rule is not vacuous there either, and the rule was run against the guide's pre-fix bytes, where it is the only one that reddens. Intent: a class the guide can drift in has to be held by something that walks the origin, and «the page declares what the origin declares» was never going to reach a number the page works out for itself.

MEASURED by extracting all twenty-five copies, substituting each demo's own name and grouping: **FOUR distinct forms — thirteen a silent ternary, six printing the operator's /etc/hosts hint with an em dash, four with a double hyphen, two without the parentheses.** The hint is real help — it is the only place the run says how to reach the vanity name — and which of the four an operator met was an accident of which task they ran, not a decision anybody made. **All twenty-five are now one form: the parenthesised em-dash spelling that prints.** That direction was chosen over the silent majority deliberately. Going silent would have deleted operator guidance that three demos' `before-after.md` transcripts publish and two of them explain in prose, and the thirty-one-line design header four of these sites carry already reasons about «the printed /etc/hosts line» — the printing form is the one the documented design describes. All eight demos' full CI task lists were run task by task and the e2e harness with them. - 2026-09-07: **the demo code explained its own history to the reader, and the rule against that had drifted back (K-1322).** The rule the project owner recorded — a comment explains the CODE, never its HISTORY — was measurably not held: over the ninety-two files that rule's row declared, comment lines were back to 39.1 per cent of the artefact against the 36.2 per cent its fix left behind, and the seven `config/initializers/kiosk.rb` — the first file an adopting operator opens, and outside that row's scope entirely — ran at two thirds comment.
