Comments arguing with a sibling demo that this file's behaviour does not depend on. Paragraphs of provenance — who measured what, which wave changed what, which row commissioned a red-team beat — rewritten as the live rule they imply. Ledger ids on lines that are not `TODO`/`FIXME`, which is the only place the constitution asks for one: **from seven hundred and eight occurrences over a hundred and fifty distinct ids down to twenty-one occurrences over five ids**, and every one of those twenty-one is PINNED — seven copies of `config/routes.rb` carry `(T-074 = A)` inside a claim `bin/check-demo-copies` holds verbatim, nine getgrocery assertion labels and one `room_hold.rb` sentence are anchored by `claims.md`, and the rest are machine-read `derived:` declarations. **What stayed, which is the half a careless sweep destroys.** Every trap that will bite the next editor: `Arel.sql` returning a String subclass so a bound function TEXT casts to NULL and matches no row; ActiveRecord casting a malformed uuid to NULL rather than raising; `insert!` and not `create!` because the rescue below depends on which exception arrives; `casecmp?` and not `==` because Postgres compares uuids canonically; `pick`/`find_by` and not the bang forms because a 404 tells a prober the id exists; the multi-process `pow_spent_store` warning in all seven initializers, in full, because a replayed proof is silent; skooti's Ed25519 unlock contract; the red-team suites' oracle discipline and their watched-fail recipes; every `NOT ROUTABLE` notice, every YARD tag and every `§`-numbered spec requirement. **The volume number moved much less than the archaeology did, and that is the finding rather than a shortfall to hide:** over the declared scope the ratio went to 38.7 per cent, and over every demo `.rb`/`.rake` in the tree to 39.3 per cent from 39.7. Eight independent passes over the same corpus each reached the same place — what is left is comment that explains the code, and cutting further would mean deleting warnings to improve a ratio, which is a worse artefact than the ratio. **No executable byte changed anywhere:** the Ripper token stream minus comment tokens is identical to the previous head for every one of the ninety-seven Ruby files this wave touched. Intent: the demo an operator copies should read as code with reasons, not as a ledger with code in it.

- 2026-09-07: **the demos' readiness poll rescued three errors, and a server that is still booting can raise at least three more (K-1352).** Thirty-seven sites across seven demos' `lib/tasks/demo.rake` and the two `script/prove_broker_boot.rb` helpers wrap a `Net::HTTP.get_response` against a process that was spawned moments earlier, retrying for forty seconds until it answers 200. Each rescued exactly `Errno::ECONNREFUSED, Errno::EADDRNOTAVAIL, SocketError` — which covers a port nothing is listening on, and not a half-open socket that accepts and then goes away. `Errno::ECONNRESET` and `EOFError` are both reachable while a Puma worker is still coming up, and `Net::OpenTimeout` is reachable on a slow one — and that last is a `Timeout::Error`, NOT a `SocketError`, which is the same taxonomy mistake K-1318 recorded one construct over about `Resolv::ResolvTimeout`. Nothing was FALSE here: no comment claimed the list was complete, so this was an incomplete rescue rather than a wrong one. The consequence is the same either way — the poll stops retrying and aborts the rake task, intermittently, which is the hardest kind of CI flake to attribute to a cause. All thirty-seven now rescue `StandardError`: a loop whose entire job is «not ready yet, try again» has no business distinguishing one way of not being ready from another, and forty seconds later it aborts with the log path whatever the reason was. **The second half of that row is answered rather than done, and the answer is a measurement.** It asked whether `lib/tasks/` should enter `bin/check-demo-copies`' scan roots, because the host-detection block is hand-copied twenty-five times with nothing comparing the copies. It should not, and the reason is that the change would do NOTHING: the scan globs `**/*.rb`, and `ruby -e 'p Dir["kiosk-demo-*/lib/tasks/**/*.rb"]'` answers `[]` — every demo's `lib/tasks/` holds exactly one file and it is `demo.rake`. Making it bite would mean globbing `.rake` too and then comparing whole rake files that are per-demo by construction, which is the signal-drowning the scan-roots comment declines in writing. The mechanism that fits is the one that file already names — a declared CONSTRUCT, the way the K-1046 flow-runner rule holds a fragment inside an uncompared file. That was not written here because its premise is false as measured: the twenty-five copies are NOT byte-identical modulo the demo name, they are FOUR distinct forms (thirteen resolve silently through a ternary; six print the fallback hint with an em dash; four print it with a double hyphen; two print it without the surrounding parentheses), so a lockstep rule would be red on arrival and the normalisation it needs is a change to twelve demos' printed output that no row has asked for. Filed as a new finding rather than folded in. Intent: a readiness loop should retry until the deadline, and a guard should be added to the mechanism that can actually hold it.

- 2026-09-07: **a rescue that could not fire on the failure it exists for, on seventeen of twenty-five host-detection sites, and a local demo run that queried the production domain on every invocation (K-1318).** Every task in four demos' `lib/tasks/demo.rake` opens by resolving its own `<demo>.demo.kiosk.tech`: if the name answers `127.0.0.1` the run uses it — which is what an operator's `/etc/hosts` entry produces, and the one branch the block exists to offer — and otherwise it falls back to `127.0.0.1`. Two independent defects. **First, the fallback could not catch the failure it is written for.** `Resolv::ResolvTimeout` is NOT a `Resolv::ResolvError`: MEASURED on the shipped interpreter, its ancestors are `[Resolv::ResolvTimeout, Timeout::Error, RuntimeError, StandardError]` and `Resolv::ResolvTimeout <= Resolv::ResolvError` is `nil`, so `rescue Resolv::ResolvError` — the spelling on six getgrocery sites, six hoteling and five skooti — let a resolver timeout out of the rake task instead of falling back. A closed network or a black-holed resolver is exactly the case a fallback to loopback is FOR, and no resolver timeout is configured anywhere here, so the stall before the raise is whatever the platform default is. atablefor's eight sites used the `rescue ""` modifier, which is `StandardError`, and were already correct; all twenty-five now name `StandardError` explicitly. **Second, and this is what a reviewer actually noticed: a purely LOCAL run made an outbound DNS query to the project's production domain, every single time, for a value it discards on any machine without the hosts entry.** The lookup is now opt-in behind `KIOSK_DEMO_HOST_LOOKUP=1`; without it nothing here touches a resolver and the run dials `127.0.0.1`, which is what CI and a fresh checkout got out of the lookup anyway — CI has working DNS and no hosts entry, which is also why CI never exercised the raising path. The printed hint moved with the behaviour, because «add this to /etc/hosts» on its own no longer changes anything and an instruction that does not do what it says is worse than none: it now names the variable as well as the entry. **That line is quoted in three published `before-after.md` recordings**, so each was re-spelled in place and each document now declares the re-spelling in its own preamble — the run was not repeated and nothing was fabricated, and `bin/check-demo-derivations` holds the new line to a literal the current task prints, which is what makes those notes checkable. Watched, on hoteling with the real task: with a `raise` planted inside the gated branch, the DEFAULT run passes (the branch is not entered) and the `KIOSK_DEMO_HOST_LOOKUP=1` run ALSO passes, because `StandardError` swallows the RuntimeError and the fallback happens — then restoring the old `rescue Resolv::ResolvError` over the same plant aborts `demo:book` at exit 1, naming the line. That pair is the defect and the fix in one measurement. Intent: a fallback should be able to fire on the failure it was written for, and running a demo on a laptop should not tell a DNS server about it.

- 2026-09-07: **eleven shipped surfaces stated a false reason for a line that no longer needs one (K-1333).** Seven demo `Gemfile`s carried a byte-identical comment saying `kiosk-server` «lazily requires json_schemer only when the flag is on», `kiosk-server`'s own `Gemfile` a staler twin calling it «NOT a runtime dependency of the gem», and four published `before-after.md` narratives told an adopter that `json_schemer` «is required only because this origin turns `c.validate_requests` on». All eleven are false at head and have been since 0.4: `VerbController#arguments_for` calls `RequestValidation.validate_arguments!` behind NO flag at all — §8.1 item 5 makes coerce-then-validate an operator obligation and `input_schema` is required on every 0.4 verb — so EVERY origin needs a JSON Schema validator, the gemspec declares it with `add_dependency`, and `validate_requests` is the separate opt-in PoW-SHAPE check that cannot switch it off. That conflation is the same one K-1336 turned out to rest on, and K-931 had already fixed this exact story in the `ConfigurationError` message without sweeping the comments, because nothing gates Gemfile comment text. The eight `gem "json_schemer"` lines are KEPT rather than deleted — they are redundant with the transitive dependency, and deleting them would move eight lockfiles for a comment fix — and every comment now says the true reason: a runtime dependency, pinned so a vendored checkout is explicit about it. The four narratives now say `json_schemer` is not one of the demo's kiosk gems and is not optional either. **The fenced Gemfile snippets in those documents were NOT touched and did not need to be:** `bin/check-demo-derivations` holds each quoted line to a line of the file it quotes, and the comments it quotes are not among them — the false sentences were the documents' own PROSE, beside the block rather than inside it, which is precisely the half a membership check cannot reach. That guard was confirmed live on this corpus by planting a `require: false` into getgrocery's quoted line and watching it name the file and the line. Intent: a comment explaining why a dependency is there should not be the last place a reader learns something the gemspec contradicts.

- 2026-09-07: **the install pointers were held by nothing, and one shipped source comment still taught the command that 404s (K-1343).** K-1319 stated publication status once, in the monorepo README's Install section, and pointed eleven gem READMEs at it — but the rule that came with it (`INSTALL_SOURCE`, in `bin/check-onboarding-derivation`) holds the PUBLISHED PAGE, not those READMEs. MEASURED: `git grep -l "Not on RubyGems yet" -- '*/README.md'` returns 11 files and the same over `bin` returns 0, so a new gem README carrying an install snippet and no pointer passed every gate in the repository, and the pointers themselves were eleven copies of a convention nobody was watching. The residue was already there: `kiosk-all/lib/kiosk-all.rb` — a file that ships INSIDE the built gem — opened with «`bundle add kiosk-all` pulls in kiosk-core + kiosk-server», an instruction that answers 404 for the one reader who has that file and no repository around it. That comment now names the Install section instead. `bin/check-gem-packaging` gains rule C to hold the class: a PACKAGED file that teaches an install of a Kiosk gem — a `gem "kiosk-…"` line, a `bundle add`, a `gem install` — must point at that section, and the pointer is DERIVED (the canonical line's `github:` slug plus the README's own Install heading) rather than typed, so the day the gems are published and the qualifier comes off, the rule FAILS asking a human to restate it rather than matching nothing and passing. Thirteen packaged files are its subjects today. A mention that is prose about the resolver rather than an instruction — kiosk-server's `ConfigurationError` rationale, which names what `gem install kiosk-server` resolves in order to explain a MISSING dependency — is declared in `INSTALL_MENTION_ONLY` with its reason and goes stale loudly, like every other manifest in that file. **The polarity is the part that needed care and is written into the file:** rules A and B are BANS, where nothing-matched is a clean tree; C is a PRESENCE requirement, where nothing-matched is the rule not running, so a run finding NO packaged file that teaches an install fails rather than passing. **Where it did NOT go, and why:** the obvious home was `bin/check-publication-paths`, whose subject is shipped lines pointing at material a reader cannot resolve — but that script is a forbidden-token table, byte-identical across both published repositories and re-recorded by digest, and a presence rule about gem READMEs has no subject at all in the other repository. A rule that can only ever match nothing in half the places it lives is the shape this workspace keeps re-discovering. Seven planted breaks were watched red and restored from file copies with the digests re-verified: a README losing its pointer, packaged Ruby gaining an unpointed `bundle add`, the canonical line losing its qualifier, the Install heading renamed, a stale exemption, the detector switched off, and the old `kiosk-all.rb` comment put back. Intent: the first command a stranger runs should work, and where it cannot yet, every file that ships should say where to find out — checked, not remembered.

- 2026-09-07: **the guard whose entire subject is hand-kept inventories drifting carried a hand-typed count of itself, and it had been wrong twice (K-1340).** `bin/check-demo-copies` holds many independent invariants behind one exit code, and its header stated how many as a cardinal. The first cardinal was a count of `# ── … ──` banners that missed a rule written with a different banner; the correction that replaced it announced an «honest total» of its own and was overtaken within days, so the file came to carry two wrong counts of itself plus a note explaining why one of them was wrong — the exact failure the file exists to prevent, committed inside the file. The count has been derivable since K-1267 built the coverage table from the banners: `--self-test --list` prints it on its first line, and `--self-test` prints it again in its summary. So the header now says the count is DERIVED, names the command, and tells the next author not to copy the number back up — and the three further self-referential cardinals in the same file (the `--self-test` rationale's «with nineteen of them», and «fifteen of the twenty rules have an in-band vacuity guard and only four of those guards have an arm here») are restated as questions `--list` answers, since it marks a break arm `B` and a vacuity arm `V`. The identical typed cardinal in the CI step that RUNS this self-test moved with them, because a wrong number one file away is still the wrong number. The earlier NINETEEN sentence is deliberately LEFT AS WRITTEN: an archived ledger row anchors it, and breaking a frozen citation to fix a live one trades one defect for another. Intent: a file that exists to stop somebody counting by hand should not be counting itself by hand.

- 2026-09-07: **two demos published a bare wall clock with no clock named, which is the exact sentence a customer misreads (K-1347).** atablefor's `availability` rows carried `seating_time: "19:00"` and getgrocery's delivery windows a `label` of `"08:00–10:00"`. Both are the field a human is actually read out — an assistant says «there is a table at 19:00», not «there is a table at 2026-09-08T19:00:00+01:00» — and neither said whose 19:00 it was. The instant beside them has carried its resolved offset all along; that is not the gap. The gap is that nobody says an offset out loud, so the one field anybody repeats to a person was the one field with no zone in it, and a customer two hours east hears their own evening. getgrocery's label is now `"08:00–10:00 (Europe/Dublin)"` and atablefor's rows gain `seating_label` — `"20:00 (Europe/Lisbon)"` — on both `availability` and `my_bookings`, built once in `Seatings.label` so every verb that publishes a seating publishes the same sentence. `book_table` echoes the caller's own `date`/`time` back rather than offering a new one, so it gains no field; its descriptor now names the zone those two are in, and points at `seating_label` for the spelled-out form. Both zone names are read from the demos' existing `ZONE_NAME` constants rather than typed a second time. **What this deliberately is NOT:** no structured `timezone` member, no request header, no caller-zone conversion — the wire mechanism for times is an open design and this is the half of it that needs no decision, because a row that states a time should say which clock it is in under every option that design could take. Intent: the field a human hears out loud should be true on its own, without arithmetic the assistant has to remember to do.

- 2026-09-07: **one getgrocery row used the word `zone` for two different things, and an assistant reading the wire could not tell which (K-1346).** `delivery_slots` published `zone: "D02"` — a Dublin POSTAL DISTRICT, a routing key — while `DeliverySlots.zone` in the same demo, in the same file family, is an `ActiveSupport::TimeZone`. One word, two meanings, and the collision sits in the worst possible place: a row that carries a delivery WINDOW is exactly where a reader expects `zone` to name the clock the window is written in. Inside the app the ambiguity is harmless, because a human knows which file they are in; on the wire the field name is all an assistant has. The published field is now `district`, its description says «a ROUTING key, not a time zone», and the internal IANA accessor keeps the name `zone` — so the two things are spelled differently at the boundary where it matters and identically nowhere. The reference driver reads and prints `district=`, and the narrative document's snippet blocks moved with the source; the one token of its 2026-08-26 transcript that the driver no longer prints was re-spelled rather than re-recorded, and the document now says so in its own preamble instead of letting `bin/check-demo-derivations` be the only place that knows. This is free today and expensive later: `GOAL.md` records that there is no backwards-compatibility constraint until Show HN, so a rename now costs nothing that a rename after adoption would. Intent: an operator's row should not spend the reader's only naming budget on a word it uses for something else.

- 2026-09-07: **stylish read a zoneless `slot` in whatever zone the server process happened to run in, and the sentence beside it said the opposite (K-1345).** `book_appointment` parsed its `slot` with stdlib `Time.iso8601`, which binds a string carrying no offset to the process `TZ`; measured, the same `"2026-09-14T14:00:00"` resolves `+11:00` under `TZ=Etc/GMT-11` and `-02:00` under `TZ=Etc/GMT+2` — one instant name, two instants, thirteen hours apart, from an environment variable nobody sets deliberately. The comment two lines down told the reader the value was «read in the app's own zone (UTC here)», which was false at head and is the reason the defect was invisible: three of the four zone-less demos leave `config.time_zone` commented and therefore genuinely do answer in UTC, so the false sentence read like a statement of the fleet's shared behaviour. stylish is the demo whose service is rendered at the PROVIDER — a chair, at an address, at an hour — so a reinterpreted instant books the wrong hour and answers with the wrong one too. The parse moves into `app/models/salon_clock.rb`, which names the salon's IANA zone (`Europe/Paris`) once, the shape atablefor's `Seatings` and hoteling's `WireArguments` already use for their own service places, and parses in TWO steps that do two different jobs: stdlib `Time.iso8601` stays as the SHAPE gate with its value deliberately discarded, because `ActiveSupport::TimeZone#iso8601` alone accepts `"12345"` (measured: 2012-12-10) and a bare `"2026-09-14"` (midnight) and would turn a malformed slot into a plausible appointment; `zone.iso8601` then computes the value, so an offset-bearing string stays absolute and a zoneless one is read at the salon. **The test is the part that matters, and it runs twice.** A single run cannot see this class at all — on the machine that wrote the code the process zone and the intended zone agree and every assertion passes — so `demo:clock_spec` runs the new DB-free `spec/salon_clock_spec.rb` under `TZ=Etc/GMT-11` and `TZ=Etc/GMT+2`, pinning a zoneless slot to an absolute epoch, checking three offset-bearing spellings of that instant agree with it, checking January answers CET rather than a frozen `+02:00`, and checking the shape gate still refuses what it always refused. Watched failing: with the stdlib parse restored as the value, four assertions go red under BOTH `TZ` values (exit 1), and the file restored from its copy returns 0. Intent: the hour a salon books should be a property of the salon, not of the box the app happens to be deployed on.

- 2026-09-07: **a reputation or PoW policy that branches on `:action` never fires, and until now no demo in the fleet exercised the branch that traps (K-1329).** The vocabulary an operator DECLARES a handler in is `kind :query` / `kind :action`; the vocabulary `Policy#challenge_for` RECEIVES is `Kiosk::Server::Executor::VERBS` — `%i[query run pay]` — so an action arrives as `:run` and `pay` is a kind of its own. `:query` is the one word spelled identically in both, which is exactly what hides the mismatch: all four demo policy hooks branched on `verb == :query`, the one predicate that means the same thing on both sides, so the seven worked examples covered the half that cannot go wrong and none of them covered the half that can. A wrong branch is SILENT — returning nil is the ordinary «do not toll this one» answer, so `verb == :action` produces no error, no log line and no failing test while the toll simply stops applying to writes, which is the direction that matters. The mapping was written down exactly once, in `kiosk-reputation/README.md`, and the normative spec said nothing. `kiosk-demo-hoteling`'s browse policy now tolls `:run` at a flat one proof (a hold on real inventory is priced differently from reading, and `:pay` is deliberately untolled), `script/browse_flow.rb` drives a real `reserve_room` through it, and `demo:browse` asserts the un-proofed action is 402-with-challenges and 200 once they are solved — a 200 on the first request is exactly what the broken spelling produces, so the assertion IS the discriminator. The protocol's Reputation section and the narrative specification now state the mapping too, so the gem README is no longer the only place it exists. Intent: an operator's first custom policy should not be able to be silently inert on writes, and the fleet should contain a worked example of the branch that traps rather than seven of the branch that does not.

- 2026-09-07: **two of the three payment-capable demos published `payment_setup`, told an assistant to call it before `pay`, and never called it themselves (K-1327).** In `kiosk-demo-hoteling` and `kiosk-demo-skooti` the verb was declared, served, and asserted PRESENT in `/kiosk/schema` by a catalogue check — and no flow driver in either issued the request, so its poll cadence, its stop condition and its ready payload were never executed in CI or anywhere while both descriptors told an assistant «the assistant should call this before `pay`». getgrocery's three flows already called it. Both drivers now do, UNCONDITIONALLY rather than inside the `SKIP_PAY` branch, because the question the verb answers is «does this principal have a card on file» — a property of the principal, not of the payment about to happen — so the payment-gate negative run exercises it too and a «ready» that only appeared when a pay followed would have nowhere to hide. Under each demo's stub PSP it is one request and an immediate `{status: "ready"}`; the cost is one request per run and the gain is that a presence assertion became a behaviour assertion. Intent: a verb an origin publishes and instructs an assistant to call should be a verb something here has actually called.

- 2026-09-07: **hoteling's search clamp was wrong at its LOWER bound, and three published sentences agreed with each other against the code (K-1328).** `search_hotels` mapped `limit=0` and every negative integer to the DEFAULT page size — twenty rows for a caller who asked for zero — while the verb's `description`, its `HINT_SEARCH_LIMIT` refusal hint, and, normatively, the descriptor house style published on kiosk.tech (whose worked hotel-search example carries the same sentence verbatim as the model operators are told to copy) all say the value is CLAMPED to 1..50 and never refused. Twenty is a THIRD behaviour that neither bound describes, and three surfaces agreeing is what put the code on the wrong side of the disagreement, so the code moved: `limit = 1 if limit < 1`. The reason it survived is that nothing anywhere sent a value below 1 — not the demo, not the e2e harness, and `validate_responses` checks payload shape rather than this — so `script/search_flow.rb` now probes `limit=0`, `limit=-5` and `limit=500` and `demo:search` asserts one row, one row and fifty, with `X-Total-Count` still counting the whole matching set so a clamped PAGE is not mistaken for a shrunken RESULT. Intent: the clamp an operator copies out of the house style should be the clamp the reference origin performs, at both ends.

- 2026-09-07: **the onboarding-derivation guard reached one verb and the page had drifted on the two next to it — and the guard's own `required:` reader was looking at the wrong list (K-1338, K-1339).** `bin/check-onboarding-derivation` derived everything from getgrocery's `delivery_slots`, so the WRITE verbs beside it were uncovered: the published page required `delivery_date` on both `create_order` and `reschedule_delivery` where getgrocery requires it on neither (omitting it books tomorrow, for the same reason the read verb leaves its own `date` optional — the caller cannot compute the operator's today), and the page's `create_order` indexed every cart entry without checking it is a Hash, so `items: ["bread"]` raised inside the handler and reached the caller as a 500 about the operator's server where getgrocery's own guard answers a typed 400 naming the shape it wanted. Three rules answer it, all derived and all scoped per verb: WRITE_REQUIRED holds every verb both sides declare to getgrocery's own `required:` list in BOTH directions (requiring too much and too little are two defects, and a parameter the page does not declare at all is neither — the page teaches a deliberately smaller surface); ITEM_SHAPE derives every input parameter getgrocery declares as an array of objects and requires each page handler that reads one to guard the element; and REQUIRE_OPTIONAL, which used to scan the whole page for one verb's optional arguments, is now computed and enforced verb by verb — a union would have been red for requiring `delivery_address` on `create_order`, which is correct, because `reschedule_delivery` leaves the same name optional. **The first of those found a bug in the guard rather than only on the page:** `required_list` returned the FIRST `required:` in a verb's region, which for `create_order` is the ITEM schema's `required: ["sku", "qty"]` two braces down — both sides answered with that nested list, so the comparison was two copies of one irrelevant fact and passed on a page that required an argument getgrocery leaves optional. `top_level_required` walks brace depth instead. Every new rule was proven by planting its defect in the working tree, running the gate bare and watching it go red; the self-test is 25 arms in both modes (13 planted breaks, 12 vacuity), and its fixture grew a write surface so the two new rules cannot pass by being vacuous against it. Intent: a guard that covers one verb of a page teaches its reader that the page is covered.

- 2026-09-06: **fourteen gemspecs promised Ruby `>= 3.2.0` and nothing had ever run on it; CI now does, and the version is read out of a gemspec rather than typed into the workflow (K-1321).** Every `setup-ruby` step in `ci.yml` pinned 4.0.1, with a matrix over `gem` and `demo` and no Ruby dimension at all — so a published gem's advertised floor was a claim its own CI contradicted by omission, and eight shipped demo READMEs advertise that same floor because `bin/check-demo-prereqs` derives it from the gemspec. The other honest repair was to RAISE the declared floor to what CI runs; it was rejected on the numbers, because `~> 8.1` railties itself declares `>= 3.2.0`, so 3.2 is the framework's own floor rather than our invention, while a floor of 4.0 would exclude essentially every existing Rails app — the audience the onboarding page addresses — in order to make a CI pin true. MEASURED before the leg was written, on Ruby 3.2.0 exactly, against a local Postgres: all fourteen suites green, 2,526 rspec examples and 27 minitest runs, zero failures. The floor was real; it had simply never been run. The new `ruby-floor` job derives the version from the gemspecs at job time and fails if they do not all declare the same one, then asserts the interpreter it actually got IS that version — because a resolver that cannot satisfy a request exactly is the one way this leg could run a different Ruby and still report success. **No toolchain pin becomes tracked, and that is now a written decision:** `.ruby-version` and `mise.toml` stay gitignored per developer, `.gitignore` says why, and the README states the supported RANGE — which is worth more to a contributor than one pinned patch a version manager they do not use will silently ignore, now that both ends of the range are on the merge gate. Intent: a floor a published gemspec promises should be a version something runs, not a number nobody has tried.

- 2026-09-06: **the onboarding guide's version floors and its install source are now DERIVED from this repository rather than typed on a page in another one (K-1320, K-1319).** `bin/check-onboarding-derivation` gains three rules and grows from seven to ten. RAILS_FLOOR reads the `~> MAJOR.MINOR` the engine's gemspec declares for all four Rails components it names — and reports itself vacuous rather than choosing if they ever disagree — and holds the published page to it; RUBY_FLOOR does the same for `required_ruby_version`, off the same gemspec `bin/check-demo-prereqs` derives the eight demo READMEs' Ruby line from, so the page and those READMEs cannot drift apart; INSTALL_SOURCE reads the source qualifier off the canonical `gem "kiosk-all", …` line in the monorepo README and requires every `gem "kiosk-*"` line the page teaches to carry it. Each has a vacuity arm that FAILS when its derivation stops computing — including the day the gems are published and the qualifier comes off, which is a moment for a human to restate the rule and not a moment to pass quietly. All twenty self-test arms fire, in both modes: against the sibling checkout and against the built-in fixture the push-blocking CI half uses. Intent: a version floor an operator is told to clear should be the one the gemspec actually declares, checked by a machine, and not a number two repositories apart that agreed once.

- 2026-09-06: **four different install instructions across the two published repositories, two of them resolving against a registry that has none of these gems, and nothing said so in either direction (K-1319, K-1320).** MEASURED 2026-09-06 against the RubyGems API: all fourteen gem names answer HTTP 404, with a control of `rails` → 200; there is no release tag in this repository and no gemspec sets `allowed_push_host`, and four Gemfiles here already carry the comment «hasn't shipped to RubyGems yet». So the top-level README's `bundle add kiosk-all` and every bare `gem "kiosk-…"` line in ten gem READMEs were instructions that cannot resolve, and a first-thirty-minutes reader found that out empirically. Publication status and the canonical install — one `github:` line for the meta-gem — are now stated ONCE, in the monorepo README, and every other surface carries a pointer to it rather than a second copy of the fact. The same section states the preconditions an adopting app must clear, which were the other half of the same silence: **Rails `~> 8.1`, which excludes 7.x and 8.0.x** — most «existing Rails apps», and until now named once anywhere, as a dependency description rather than as a precondition — plus the Ruby floor and Postgres. No toolchain pin is tracked and that is now a written decision rather than an omission: the README states the supported range and CI runs both ends of it, which is worth more to a contributor than one pinned patch a version manager may ignore. Intent: the first command a stranger runs should work, and where it cannot yet, the repository should say so itself instead of letting bundler say it.

- 2026-09-06: **CORRECTION to the 2026-08-12 routes entry below, which stands as written: the migration it promised was never performed (K-1314).** That entry says the demos and the e2e fixture keep their hand-drawn routes byte-identical «until T-057 migrates them», and T-057 was closed `done` without touching a route file. MEASURED at head with `git grep "mount Kiosk"` (BRE) over the pathspec `'kiosk-demo-*/config/routes.rb' 'e2e/fixtures/routes.rb'`: **exit 1, zero hits across all nine files** — every demo and the e2e fixture still hand-draw the surface the engine has owned since K-505, which is the escape hatch, while `onboarding.html` tells an adopting operator to mount unless they have a reason not to. Nothing noticed because nothing looks: `bin/check-demo-copies` records `config/routes.rb` in `SKELETON_NOT_COMPARED`, and no gate asserts a mount anywhere. History here is append-only, so the earlier entry is superseded by this one rather than edited, and the migration itself is tracked as T-174. Intent: a promise this file makes about the tree has to be true of the tree, and where it has stopped being true the correction belongs in the file a reader is reading.

- 2026-09-06: **this file's own header described a file that has never existed, and now describes this one — plus the one fact per entry that IS held (K-1330).** The header claimed «one line per change, 1–2 sentences» while the 461 entries under it measured a median of 776 characters, p90 1,329 and a longest of 4,350, with 19 of the 461 short enough (≤250 characters) to be what it asked for: a rule its own file contradicts ninety-six times in a hundred teaches a reader to skip headers, and this header is on the file where K-1314's false migration promise sat unread. The header now says what this file is — the repository's dated engineering journal — and points the short per-release shape at each gem's own `CHANGELOG.md`, which is inside the published package and is what an installer reads. Entry LENGTH is deliberately still ungated: a length census over 461 standing entries would be red on arrival, and a gate that reddens on a backlog is a gate someone switches off. What IS gated is the file's only ordering affordance: `bin/check-changelog` gains arm CL-7, which fails on a top-level entry of the repository record that does not open `- YYYY-MM-DD: `, scoped to that file because a gem record carries its date on the release heading instead. Two entries had lost their date among 459 that had one; both were dated from the commits that introduced them and nothing else about either was touched. Intent: a file that ships in a public repository should describe itself accurately, and the part of it a machine can hold should be held by the machine.

- 2026-09-06: **a fourth wire-facing route stopped publishing a library's sentence, and the sweep that documented it as out of reach now holds it (K-1310).** `include Kiosk::Handler` maps a Rails-native raise onto a wire code with no Kiosk classes in the handler — and it was rendering that exception's own message as the problem document's `detail`, so a `params.require(:sku)` handler answered a 400 reading actionpack's «param is missing or the value is empty or invalid: sku». That branch exists only for exceptions the operator did NOT author, so the caller now gets a Kiosk sentence and a `hint` per code while the exception's class, message and backtrace go to the operator's log, the way the two 500 paths already send theirs; the priced cost is that a host registering its own class in `rescue_responses` loses that sentence too, and the two documented ways to speak to an assistant — render the envelope, raise a Kiosk error — are pinned by name beside the loss. The engine's own error sweep now walks `render` as a second construction, because a scope note that names a live leak documents it instead of holding it. Intent: everything an assistant reads when it is refused should be something this protocol chose and can version.

- 2026-09-06: **an operator's audit sink gets the handler's own exception back, beside the wire's wrapper rather than instead of it (K-1311).** Dropping the handler's sentence from the wire also dropped it from `c.audit_sink`, which had been receiving the cause and started receiving `Action "place_order" raised RuntimeError` — and a sink is operator-side, in the operator's own process, already holding the arguments in full, so there was nothing there to protect it from. `ActionEvent` now carries `cause_class` and `cause_message` untruncated alongside the two error fields, read off the `cause` Ruby attaches automatically, so a sink alerting on `error_class` keeps meaning what it meant. Intent: the wire and the operator's own trail are different audiences, and a redaction chosen for one should not silently apply to the other.

- 2026-09-06: **`bin/rails g kiosk:install` now writes the validation posture the demos ship, instead of leaving a fresh app on the engine defaults (K-1336).** The generated initializer set neither flag, so a new origin got no PoW-shape check — a malformed proof retries into a silent 402 loop with no diagnostic — and no output validation at all, while all seven demos set both; it now carries `validate_requests = true` and, matching K-1332, `validate_responses = !Rails.env.production?`, each with the reason beside it. It also says outright what those flags are NOT: per-verb coerce-then-validate against `input_schema` is unconditional and behind no flag, which is a conflation the gemspec's own wording had already caused once. Intent: the file we generate should start an adopter where we would want them, and should not let a reader mistake an opt-in for an obligation.

- 2026-09-06: **the end-to-end harness executed shell commands out of the comments it wrote into a generated Gemfile (K-1337).** `e2e/run.sh` built that file through a heredoc whose delimiter was UNQUOTED, so the shell command-substituted every backticked span in the BODY before writing it: two comments reached the generated Gemfile mangled, two `command not found` lines went to stderr on every run, and the harness exited 0 throughout — which is why nobody saw it for as long as it existed. The delimiter is quoted now and the one value that must vary is spliced in afterwards by a literal substitution, so nothing in that block is executable ever again; the harness's other heredocs were audited in the same pass and the one that stays unquoted says why. Intent: prose in a file we generate is prose, and a gate that writes to stderr under an exit 0 is a gate whose output nobody reads.

- 2026-09-06: **six demos depended on an archived gem for a test that could not do anything (K-1331).** `UuidCheck.valid?` ANDed its own canonical-uuid pattern with the `uuid` gem's validator, which accepts a strict superset of that pattern — so the conjunction was extensionally the pattern alone, and the price was an archived dependency plus two transitive ones in six lockfiles, on a repository where a dependency audit is the first thing a reader runs. Re-measured before removal over 100,000 random candidates and eighteen edge spellings with zero disagreements, and over 200,000 canonical-shaped strings the gem rejected none of. Intent: a public reference should not carry a dependency that does nothing, and the reason it did nothing belongs next to the code rather than in a ledger.

- 2026-09-06: **the two development keypairs this repository tracks are now DECLARED rather than merely present, and a third one can no longer arrive unnoticed (K-1334).** Nothing in any gate carried a secret pattern and CI runs no scanner, so the next committed key would have shipped in silence. `bin/check-publication-paths` — byte-identical in both published repositories — now fails on any tracked private key outside two declared paths, and on either of those two if it loses its do-not-use banner; because one of those repositories contains no key at all, the rule proves its detector against a freshly minted key on every run instead of trusting a count. The two keys stay: renaming them changes nothing a scanner does, the Ed25519 one is a known-answer vector reproduced in lock firmware and a C host test, and both entered history in commits already published, which this project does not rewrite. `.github/secret_scanning.yml` and a README section say all of that out loud. Intent: a key in a public reference implementation should be a decision someone wrote down, and the next one should cost a build.

- 2026-09-06: **an orphaned `.dockerignore` outlived two sweeps that deleted its siblings, and the class is now held rather than swept (K-1335).** It was `rails new` scaffolding whose `Dockerfile` had been dropped — a configuration file naming a build nothing in the tree can run — and two earlier passes each deleted it from the demos they happened to open. `bin/check-demo-copies` now fails on any container ignore-file with no recipe beside it or above it, and because that rule polices a tree holding zero of its subject, it proves its own detector on constructed names every run rather than reporting a count. Intent: the third instance of one defect is a missing rule, not a missing sweep.

- 2026-09-06: **a commit here can break a page published there, and now something notices — `bin/check-onboarding-derivation`, with its own CI job (K-1325, K-1324, K-1326).** `kiosk.tech/onboarding.html` carries a full controller pair as copyable Ruby, every line of it a copy of something in this repository, and nothing compared a byte: when getgrocery made `delivery_slots`' date optional the guide went on teaching `required: ["date"]` and a frozen calendar day, and was found ten hours later by a human eye rather than by a gate. The new guard derives seven facts here — the handler class names the install generator teaches, that verb's required list and parameter names, every parameter declared `type: "integer"`, the classes getgrocery's own initializer registers — and holds the page to them; run against the live site the day it was written it reddened six of its seven rules. Neither repository's CI checks the other out, so the live comparison goes over the network on the daily schedule the solver pin already uses, and the half that needs neither network nor sibling — a self-test that plants a break in every rule, into a fixture built from these same derivations when no page resolves — blocks every push. When it can reach no page at all it exits 1 saying the check did not run, rather than passing. Intent: the guide is a supply chain into somebody else's production app, and a copy nothing compares is a copy that drifts.

- 2026-09-06: **`bin/setup` — the first command a Rails developer types — aborted on its last line in four of the seven demos, and set the other three up wrong (K-1315).** Every copy ended `exec "bin/dev"` at a file only three demos shipped, and every copy ran `db:prepare` rather than the demo's own `demo:setup`, so where it did complete the app came up with no seed data and without the `app_role` grants its queries assume; tudu shipped three of its `bin/` scripts non-executable, which is the same failure spelled EACCES. `bin/setup` now runs `demo:setup`, `bin/dev` ships in all seven, and the repair is held by two mechanisms rather than by memory: both files are compared byte-for-byte with a declared demo set, and `bin/check-demo-copies` now DERIVES the question from the scripts themselves — every `bin/<name>` a demo's own scripts or its README names must resolve to an existing, executable file. Intent: the entry point an operator reaches for first should work in every demo, and should leave them with a demo they can run.

- 2026-09-06: **the documented prerequisites were not the actual prerequisites, in every demo, and CI could not have noticed (K-1316, K-1317).** Nothing an operator reads named python3 + numpy — which every task that registers an assistant needs, because registration pays an Equihash toll the bundled Python solver solves — or the `CREATE ROLE` privilege `demo:setup` needs for `app_role`; CI installs numpy and runs Postgres as a superuser, so both gaps were invisible to it by construction. Two READMEs also described `demo:setup` as "create + load schema + seed" when it drops the database unconditionally, and three omitted the drop entirely. Every demo README now carries a prerequisites block that is GENERATED from that demo's own files by `bin/check-demo-prereqs` — a requirement appears because the code triggers it, the destructive-setup warning names the database `config/database.yml` resolves, and a requirement no demo triggers any more fails the build instead of quietly covering nothing. `kiosk.tech/onboarding.html` states the same two, and states that an adopting operator needs neither. Intent: what a stranger must have installed before anything works should be derived from the code that needs it, not remembered in seven places.

- 2026-09-06: **the deployed demo fleet ran the production posture its own engine warns against (K-1332).** All seven demo initializers set `c.validate_responses = true` unconditionally while every deploy template sets `RAILS_ENV=production`, so on the public subdomains an assistant is pointed at, an operator's descriptor typo answered a 500 to a caller who had done nothing wrong — and the demo initializer is the file `onboarding.html` sends an adopting operator to copy. They now spell it `!Rails.env.production?`, which costs nothing: the task lists that make each demo a per-verb conformance proof run in development, and `deploy/production-smoke.sh` was measured to drive no verb to a success body at all, so the check never ran there. `bin/check-demo-copies` holds the line in all seven and holds it against the engine's own hazard sentence. Intent: an example an operator is told to copy should demonstrate the posture we would recommend, and a public endpoint should not answer 500 for a typo of ours.

- 2026-09-06: **a refusal now carries a sentence this protocol chose, not one a library happened to say: nine engine paths stop splicing a foreign exception message into a wire error, and each publishes a `hint` in its place (K-1307, K-1306).** The spliced text belonged to the json gem, the JWT gem, OpenSSL and Rack — it can echo the caller's own bytes back on paths reachable before any credential is presented, and it moves whenever a dependency is upgraded, so it was an undeclared and unversioned part of the wire; the two 500 paths keep the exception CLASS, which carries no caller bytes, and the message and its backtrace now go to the operator's log, where those paths' hint has promised the backtrace all along. Because this is the third recurrence of one class, the deliverable is the sweep rather than the nine edits: the engine's suite reads its whole `lib` and fails when an exception message this repository does not own reaches the constructor of anything that becomes a problem document, and the house-sentence rule that guarded interpolation now catches a literal retype of the same sentence too. Intent: what an assistant reads when it is refused should be something we decided, can version, and can be held to.

- 2026-09-06: **CORRECTION to the 2026-09-05 HSTS entry below, which stands as written: its premise is false, and this is the last live copy of it (K-1309, K-1300).** That entry says the live `/etc/caddy/Caddyfile` is hand-maintained and does not import the repository's snippet; measured the next day, `deploy/deploy-caddy.sh` installs `deploy/Caddyfile` whole — which is how HSTS went on across the fleet and the per-IP throttle came off — so edge configuration IS deployable from this repository, and the retired belief's practical form is the opposite of true. History here is append-only, so the earlier entry is superseded by this one rather than edited. Intent: this file ships, and a reader should not meet a retired belief with nothing beside it.

- 2026-09-06: getgrocery's `delivery_slots` no longer requires a `date`: omitting it asks for the soonest day the shop can deliver, which is the only form a caller can get right, because the day rolls over in the operator's locale and not in the caller's.
- 2026-09-06: **a retired brand name grew back in shipped code nine days after it was scrubbed, and now something checks (K-1296).** Two demo comments still named the KYC broker by a domain this project does not own; they name what it IS instead. The scrub itself was never the fix -- nothing anywhere forbade the class, so the next reintroduction would have been just as invisible -- so `bin/check-publication-paths` gains an arm that refuses any demo operator named at a domain outside the one real hosted domain, in BOTH published repositories, since the twin is the single-source rule. Intent: a name Phil retired should cost a build, not another sweep.

- 2026-09-06: **getgrocery's storefront stops advertising a delivery counter that no current order can move (K-1291).** The «Deliveries scheduled» stat counted a status the verb that wrote it stopped writing when `schedule_delivery` became `reschedule_delivery`; because this origin is reseeded additively it never fell to zero, so it read as live activity while sitting frozen on rows a retired verb left behind. It now counts the same predicate the admin badge and the one-reschedule-per-order rule already use. Intent: a number presented as live has to be one a request can change.

- 2026-09-06: **the fleet now sends HSTS on all eight origins, the runbook says so, and the box's actual size is written down for the first time (K-1295, T-171, T-161).** The header arrived by deploying the repo's Caddyfile rather than by pasting a line onto the box, so the runbook's HSTS and Caddy sections now describe a deploy instead of a hand-edit, and the checklist ticks that told an operator to paste are gone. The measured VPS -- 2 vCPU, 3814 MB -- is recorded beside the sizing advice that has been guessing at it, together with the fact that every throughput number in this repository was taken on a laptop with four times the cores. Intent: a runbook should describe the box we run, by a mechanism that survives the next deploy.

- 2026-09-06: **the deploy runbook no longer tells an operator to install a rate-limit module, because the fleet deliberately has no default throttle (T-171).** Phil's 2026-09-05 decision removed the per-IP edge limit; `deploy/check-edge-ratelimit.sh`, which asserted the limiter was PRESENT and printed instructions for enabling it, is retired rather than inverted -- an inverted gate would go red the day someone correctly turns the throttle back on, and the snippet is kept commented for exactly that day. The Caddyfile, the runbook and the checklist now state the no-throttle posture and the measurements behind it, and its CI slot moves to `deploy/deploy-caddy.sh --self-test`, which holds both directions of the posture. Intent: the shipped instructions should describe the configuration we actually run.

- 2026-09-06: The demo fleet's Caddy configuration is deployed from the repository instead of hand-maintained on the box: `deploy/deploy-caddy.sh` ships `deploy/Caddyfile` whole, validates it with the box's own Caddy, and rolls back if the live wire disagrees. Applying it turned HSTS on across all eight origins and took off the per-IP edge throttle, which was refusing an honest caller's eighth request.
- 2026-09-05: **the fleet has never sent HSTS, and the runbook said the Caddyfile was its source — the claim is corrected and the header now has a probe instead of a checklist line (K-1295).** MEASURED against the deployed fleet: not one origin `deploy/Caddyfile` declares answers `Strict-Transport-Security` at all, while that file's `(kioskproxy)` snippet has emitted it, enabled and needing no module, since K-916. The template was never the defect; the live `/etc/caddy/Caddyfile` is hand-maintained, serves other sites and does not import the snippet — the same split the edge rate limit has. The diagnosis is the useful half and it is now written where it can act: the rate-limit half of this class got a script and landed, HSTS got a line in a checklist that is 0 ticked of 45, so its tick state carried no information. `deploy/check-live-hsts.sh` reads the WIRE rather than a config, because a config check on the template would have said OK for as long as the box has been serving without the header, and it prints the exact line an operator pastes into each vhost. Intent: a security control whose only enforcement is somebody remembering is not enforced, and this one had been unenforced for as long as anyone had been looking at the template instead of the response.

- 2026-09-05: **`request_kyc` stops answering an unreachable KYC broker with a Ruby exception in a 500, and the two halves of the KYC module now agree on what "no KYC here" is (K-1299).** OBSERVED rather than derived, on a plainly booted origin — which is the configuration a live demo run uses and the one no gate had ever exercised, because `demo:agecheck` boots the broker and sets the intake secret: `Action "request_kyc" raised RuntimeError: KYC broker intake secret is not configured …`, and with the broker's port refused, `raised Errno::ECONNREFUSED: Failed to open TCP connection to 127.0.0.1:9 …` — the operator's own broker address, on a no-argument verb an assistant can call first. The broker client now raises two named errors instead of four bare strings, and the verb refuses `501 module_not_served` when this deployment opens no verifications at all — the same code and the same sentence the engine's `KycVerifier` already answers for the submit half — and a DELIBERATE `500 action_failed` with a retry hint when the broker simply did not complete the request, because a transient outage must not be told in a status that is cacheable by default. The diagnostic goes to the operator's log, where it belonged. getgrocery's redteam battery gained a beat that dials the verb in exactly the configuration that battery already boots. Intent: an assistant meeting this fleet must be able to tell "this operator does not do KYC" from "something crashed", and until now it could tell neither.

- 2026-09-05: **the engine stops putting Ruby exception text on the wire: an absent field is named by NAME, and a body that is not JSON is refused in this protocol's words rather than the json gem's (K-1294).** Measured live against the deployed fleet, `POST /kiosk/auth/register` with an empty object answered `missing field: key not found: :public_key` — `KeyError#message` verbatim, Ruby symbol and all, on the FIRST call any assistant makes — and a malformed body answered the parser's own `unexpected token …` sentence. Both are now built in one place and read `missing field: public_key` and `invalid JSON body`, the latter carrying the recovery advice as a `hint`; the first is the sentence the demos already answer with and the one an assistant's error handling matches on. The engine was the one producer of it that no rule in the tree could see, so the cross-demo house-sentence rule now reads the refusal off the demos' and the engine's own method bodies and compares them, and the engine's suite sweeps its whole `lib` for a site building either sentence for itself. Intent: register is step one of the skill, so the very first thing a live assistant reads off this fleet was a Ruby internal.

- 2026-09-04: **the vendored problem schema the e2e harness validates live wire bytes against now binds each error code to its HTTP status, and `Errors::CODES` is derived from it rather than restated (K-1286).** The engine's `code` -> status table and the published one were hand-kept copies on opposite sides of a repository boundary and nothing compared them; the suite's own check was a third literal, which only ever proved that someone had typed the same seventeen rows twice. Measured before the change: publishing `conflict` as 410 on every kiosk.tech surface while this table kept 409 left thirteen guards at exit 0. The expected value now comes out of the vendored schema, so a status that moves on either side reddens here, and the wire-byte conformance run enforces the same mapping against a live origin. Intent: the cross-repo half still needs both checkouts and runs in neither CI, so the honest gain is that each side is now held to one machine-readable copy instead of to a memory of the other.

- 2026-09-03: **the vendored descriptor schema the e2e harness validates live wire bytes against now refuses an UNDECLARED member on a verb description, in lockstep with the published copy (T-167, K-1278).** The catalogue root refused a stray key from the start and each element of its two arrays did not, so an origin publishing `"x_internal_sla": "4h"` on a verb passed `schema_conformance.rb` as cleanly as it passed the site's validator. It costs this fleet nothing — both registries build a descriptor from a fixed key set and the specs already assert that hash whole — which is precisely why the mirror could move the day the decision was taken. Intent: the mirror is worth exactly what the published schema refuses, and a merge gate that accepts more than the specification does is not holding the code against what implementers read.

- 2026-09-02: **an operator's role resolution is now a stated CONTRACT — total, or no roles at all — and the engine says so in the operator's log the one moment the mixture is decidable (T-165, Phil's decision on K-1134).** An identity system that names a role for staff and nothing for customers was never a supported configuration, but nothing said that anywhere an operator reads before shipping, and the consequence was silent: a binding that resolves no role leaves the assistant's existing role in place, so on a multi-role origin an assistant already holding the privileged one keeps it while its principal becomes a human who holds none. The generated initializer, the Devise adapter and the two-role demo now state the requirement, and `AccountBinding.bind!` warns — never raises — when a ceremony resolves no role on an origin declaring more than one. Nothing on the wire changes. Intent: the fleet was total by accident of configuration rather than by contract, and the operator this was decided for is one we will never see.

- 2026-08-31: **the wire stops answering three different situations with one `not_found`, and the two modules an operator can decline now say so instead of answering `forbidden` (T-158, K-1207 — Phil's decision «A»).** An unregistered verb NAME is `404 verb_not_found` whose `hint` still lists the names that exist; `not_found` narrows to the case §9.1 rule 2 already defined, an argument that ADDRESSED something absent; and an origin serving no KYC or no payment answers the new `501 module_not_served` where it used to answer a 403 that means «this identity may not», which was never what an origin-wide refusal meant. Intent: `code` is the one field the spec tells an assistant to branch on, so a fact it must react to differently has to be a different code and not a sentence in `hint`.

- 2026-08-31: **the deploy runbook's «poke it» one-liner had never been executed, and it fails on its FIRST line — the block described a register handshake this repository stopped serving (K-1277).** The filed defect was one pipe: it fed the challenge to `solve.py` on stdin where the solver reads its first ARGUMENT and answers a usage error. Run against the hosted origin exactly as written, the block fails earlier and in three further places — `/kiosk/auth/challenge` answers 400 without a `public_key`, what it returns is a proof-of-POSSESSION nonce rather than the PoW challenge, and the register body is `{public_key, signed}` with the proof in the `Kiosk-PoW` header rather than the solver's output as the body — plus a fourth that would have swallowed the rest: the token field is `access_token`, so `jq -r .token` bound the string «null» and every call after it answered 401. The block is now the real handshake, keypair through tolled query, and it was extracted from the file VERBATIM and run end to end against the hosted origin before being published. Intent: this runbook is what a Show HN reader copy-pastes first, and a documented command nobody has executed is a claim rather than an instruction.

- 2026-08-31: **the mandate `currency` domain closes on ISO 4217 alpha-3, so a human who types a currency NAME is refused before any money is touched instead of being told their card failed (T-159, K-1252).** Phil settled the choice half of the domain question with «accept iso codes»; the guard enforces the alpha-3 SHAPE rather than a list, because the only PSP integration this repository ships enumerates nothing — it documents a server-side supported set NARROWER than ISO 4217 — so a typed list would be a hand-kept enumeration that still let `xau` through to the same failure, and the numeric-3 codes ISO also publishes would guarantee one. `"Euro"`, `"US"` and `"978"` now answer 403, the status T-150 published for the mandate limb class the day before, and the example that pinned the old looseness on purpose was rewritten to assert the new boundary rather than deleted. Intent: an operator that says no should say it about the currency, not leave the payment provider to say it about the card.

- 2026-08-31: **the vendored descriptor schema the e2e harness validates live wire bytes against now refuses the withdrawn `params` key, in lockstep with the published copy (K-1275).** The withdrawal was prose on both prose surfaces and held by nothing on the formal side, so a catalog re-publishing the key validated clean here as well as on the site. Intent: the mirror exists so a merge gate can hold the code against what implementers actually read, and it is worth exactly what the published schema refuses.

- 2026-08-31: **the deploy tree told a poker the light proof-of-work toll costs «~1 s» in twelve places and it costs about a fifth of that, so the seconds are gone from every sentence that could not say what hardware produced them (K-1276).** Re-measured on the machine the one attributed sentence names: n=96 k=5 — the level six of the seven hosted origins run and the only one `e2e/` knows — is ~0.2 s p50, while that same sentence's ~9–10 s for n=168 k=7 reproduces exactly, so the claim was half right in a way no reader could have separated. Eleven audience sentences now say «sub-second», the wording the demos' own `pow_difficulty.rb` has had right all along; the two that name their hardware keep a figure and point at the bench grid, which gains the fleet's actual level because a k=7 sweep had never measured it. The gem README's ~530× lever is correct and correctly scoped, and nothing said the deployed fleet buys ~40× — it does now, derived from that file's own numbers by the spec that already holds the other one. Intent: a second in shipped prose is a claim about somebody's laptop, and the twelve sites that could not name one should never have been quoting seconds at all.

- 2026-08-31: **the descriptor's retired `params` slot leaves the wire, so the catalog stops publishing a key whose only legal value was `null` (T-085).** Phil authorised the removal in 0.4 on the condition the slot was unused; re-measured at head across all three repos, no macro declares it, no `Entry` carries it, nothing branches on its value and 0 of 49 fleet verbs publishes a non-null one, so the `params: nil` literal in both registries was the only thing keeping it there. The engine publishes the descriptor shape on TWO documents and both move together — the registries and the derived OpenAPI component — and the specs assert ABSENCE of the key rather than a nil value, because a nil-value assertion cannot tell a removed key from a published null. Intent: before Show HN a published slot can still be withdrawn; after it, it could only be deprecated, and a wire that carries a field nobody may write and nobody reads teaches an adopter to implement it.

- 2026-08-31: **the check that holds a descriptor's published examples to that descriptor's own schemas ran against the reference origin only, and the seven operator origins — which publish far richer descriptors — were held by nothing (T-097).** It found two violations on its first run inside the harness; extended to the fleet on the bytes each demo's own flow driver GETs off `/kiosk/schema`, it found stylish publishing an integer `appointment_id` where its own schema declares a uuid string — the same defect, in the demo the harness fixtures were staged from, standing for eleven days because nothing outside the harness looked. The loop lives in one file copied byte-identically into all seven and held there, so a one-sided edit fails the build, and each origin passes its own count of published examples as a floor, because a loop over an empty list asserts nothing. It also had to learn what the engine already knew: `limit` and `cursor` are reserved names the wire always accepts, so an example may legitimately show one against a closed schema, and both copies now perform the engine's own subtraction rather than restating the set. Intent: an example an assistant is invited to copy is a promise, and a promise nothing checks is where the fleet's descriptors were.

- 2026-08-31: **every place a demo restates its red-team beat list was a hand-kept copy that nothing compared to the beats that run, and the class had already come back three times after being fixed by hand (T-078, T-105/K-901, K-709, K-710).** A suite header, its `demo:redteam` rake `desc` and its README battery section each enumerate the same list in a different file, and the drift is the cheapest possible to reintroduce and the most expensive to notice, because a stale header makes a reader believe a beat runs that does not — stylish once named one that did not exist while omitting three that did, and philslist's two new beats arrived with the per-verb cutover and reached none of its prose. `bin/check-redteam-headers` derives each demo's beat set from its own registrations — four named extractors, because the seven suites are two harnesses and skooti runs nine beats outside any registry — and fails both directions: a restatement missing a beat that runs, and a restatement naming one that does not. It refuses to guess: a registration it cannot read is a loud failure rather than a short list quietly compared to a short list. Lists, never counts, on the same ground the last repair deleted its totals. It found two live omissions on its first run. Intent: a list that has to be re-typed to stay true will eventually not be, and the fourth repair should be the last one done by hand.

- 2026-08-31: **a leftover NOT NULL column with no DEFAULT is a live 500 waiting for a box of that vintage, and the migration-replay gate printed it as a note (T-132).** The verdict was right to be soft about leftovers in general — an absence 500s, a leftover does not — but wrong about that one shape, and the reason it stayed soft is that its message could not tell a table this codebase still writes to from one it retired: «every INSERT into that table is refused» is literally true and connotatively false for a table nothing inserts into, and the two audit tables retired a fortnight ago produced every one of the notes. So the verdict now reads the difference off head's own `structure.sql`, which is the only thing that can answer it and cannot go stale; a leftover on a retired table says so, in those words, and is never fatal. What is left — a wall on a table that is still live — fails by default, and the escape hatch is a declaration carrying the ledger row that made each accepted leftover accepted, not a flag somebody can leave off. Measured on the pre-rebuild window, that declaration covers all 22 surviving leftovers, which is what makes `--strict` usable for the first time. Intent: a warning nobody can act on teaches the scroll-past reflex that carries the next real one away.

- 2026-08-31: **the largest guard in this repository held twenty independent invariants behind one exit code and the only proof that any of them still reddened was a hand-planted break that left no artefact (K-1267).** Landing the last two convergence rules took nine plant-and-restore cycles across five demo files and the guard itself; every one was a fixture somebody wrote, watched go red, and threw away, so the next session inherits an exit code and no evidence — and a single reverted regex takes one invariant out of service while that exit code stays 0. `bin/check-demo-copies` now copies the checkout into throwaway trees and plants one faithful mistake per invariant — a one-sided edit, a copy appearing where no declared set expects it, an exception that has gone stale, a framework claim a sweep dropped from one document — and requires each to come back red NAMING its invariant; four further arms switch off a rule's own measurement to prove the vacuity guard watching it is alive, asserting liveness on the DECLARATIONS because a ban whose violations match nothing is the ban being obeyed. Measured while writing it: the header's count of nineteen is a count of banners and misses one, so the honest total is twenty, and the coverage table is derived from those banners rather than kept by hand. Intent: proving a gate is a gate should leave something behind.

- 2026-08-31: **the copy manifest's convergence tripwire needed UNANIMITY, so most of a per-demo group agreeing while one copy still differed was invisible — and three of `script/schema_flow.rb`'s seven copies were already one file (K-1262).** `:per_demo` means compared to nothing, and the only thing that noticed such a group had stopped differing fired when ALL its copies agreed; measured by plant, converging two of three left the build green. So the state that most reliably means somebody hand-copied something was the one state nothing could see, at either granularity. Every subset of a per-demo entry's copies that agrees once comments and whitespace are normalised away must now be DECLARED, as one artefact — then it is held in lockstep and a one-sided edit fails — or as an accident, which is recorded, capped at twelve normalised lines so it cannot become a free way to switch the rule off, and fails when a further demo joins it. atablefor, philslist and tudu's 37-line discovery driver is now held; the two pairs of `operation_result.rb` maps that coincide keep saying so. Intent: a declaration that the copies differ should be a claim the build can falsify.

- 2026-08-31: **six copies of the line that decides which Devise modules an operator's human IdP loads were compared to nothing, because the shared-unit rule had no way to say «one copy legitimately differs» (K-1263).** The file-level copy manifest has carried an `except:` map since K-643 for exactly that shape; the unit-level manifest one level down had `rule:`, `ships:` and `why:` and nothing else, so `app/models/user.rb#devise` — identical in six demos, with tudu alone adding `:registerable` because it is the one demo whose humans sign themselves up — could only be declared per-demo, which means compared to nothing at all. The unit rule now takes the same `except:` map and checks it as hard: a blank reason, an exception for a demo that no longer defines the unit, an exception whose copy has since converged on its siblings, and an entry where every copy but one is excepted all fail. Intent: a manifest that cannot state a true thing will be made to state a useless one.

- 2026-08-31: **`e2e/fixtures/` was compared to nothing by any path rule, and five separate arms had each hand-written their own way in (T-118).** The copy scanner walks `kiosk-demo-*` only, so no fixture ever entered its discovery map — which is defensible until you count what it produced: four fixers, four rediscoveries of the same dead end, and a directory where the next rule silently costs a second implementation or passes vacuously. Measured, eight of the 22 fixtures have a demo counterpart they already agree with, one of them byte-for-byte, and nothing asked whether that was intent or drift. Every fixture is now declared with the manifest's own three rules, PAIRED with the demo path `e2e/run.sh` already says it is installed at — so a fixture that moves moves with it — and the seven with no counterpart say so in a declaration that is itself tested: a demo file that becomes identical to one of them is reported as the copy it is. Intent: «the harness is not a demo» should stop meaning «the harness is not checked».

- 2026-08-31: **fourteen file headers asserted checkable facts about the tree and nothing could contradict any of them, because the rule that compares those files ignores comments by design (T-117, K-1021).** That is how one false sentence — the wire controllers' base class — lived in eight copies until a human read it. Comparing the comments instead would delete the reason the rule exists, and thirteen more bespoke assertions in K-1021's shape would leave the fourteenth unguarded, so what is held is the part of a header that the tree can answer: its CITATIONS. Every source path a comment names must exist, and every `{Constant}` or `{Constant.member}` it references must resolve against an index built from the demos, the harness and every gem — 208 and 147 of them respectively, across every `.rb` the manifest declares in every demo that ships it plus the fixtures, which is a wider corpus than the row asked for and was measured before it was chosen. It found two live ones on its first run. Intent: a header that points at something is making a claim, and a claim is the part a machine can keep.

- 2026-08-31: **the completeness-promise rule's window reached back over the previous block's own commentary, and the guard whose regexes are a phrase BAN had no way to notice one of them had stopped matching (T-129, K-1106).** Where two blocks share a section with no heading between them the nearest fence above a declaration is the EARLIER block's closing one, so a `verbatim` written about a snippet was attributed to whatever came next — measured, three blocks inherited one that way, and the row that filed this read it as harmless because the rule fires only on transcripts and underived blocks. Re-measured, that reading was wrong in one place: hoteling's underived block sits directly under a snippet and is green only because those eight lines happen to carry no promise word. The window now starts after the previous block's commentary, unless the gap holds a single paragraph that serves both — which hoteling's does, and dropping it would have deleted the rule's only live subject. Because a ban that is being obeyed matches nothing, the proof is a positive control instead of a live subject: every regex carries the sentence that put it in the list and a new `--self-test` holds it there, alongside four fixture documents for the window geometry the corpus has one instance of. Intent: a rule whose subject set is empty by design cannot be proven by running it.

- 2026-08-31: **the shared-unit rule's handle was a `def` or a constant, so shared code written in a DSL was invisible — and the path that proved it had been EXCLUDED from the rule with nothing recording that it contributed nothing (T-146).** `app/models/cart_mandate.rb` ships in three demos that share a `scope :referencing`, a `has_many :settlements` and a `self.table_name`, and the extractor found zero units in all three; the only record of that was a paragraph of header prose, which reads exactly like coverage. The handle now takes its start points from the PARSE TREE as well — every bare method call in a class or module body, named by its head, so a macro this repository has never used is reached the day it is written and nothing has to be listed. It found ten shared units the old handle had never seen, across five files, moving the fleet-wide count from 114 shared names to 124. Beside it, every `:per_demo` path is now either walked by the rule or named in a declared exclusion with a reason, a walked path that shares NOTHING is reported by name instead of counted as held, and the handle finding no class body at all is a failure rather than a quiet zero. Intent: an exclusion nobody checks is indistinguishable from a check.

- 2026-08-31: **~55 lines of the two KYC demos' broker-boot harness were shared and held by nothing, because the one method carrying them was declared per-demo (T-147).** `:per_demo` in the copy manifest means compared to NOTHING, and `ProveBrokerBoot#with_broker` normalised to 64 lines in getgrocery against 61 in skooti with 46 in common — the broker DB setup, the spawn, the at_exit teardown, the 40-second readiness poll and the yield/ensure — while the four lines that genuinely differ are all operator identity. Those four now sit in per-operator units the manifest names one by one; what is left normalises to 54 identical lines in both copies and is held there, so a one-sided edit to the poll or the teardown fails the build. Intent: an all-or-nothing declaration on a method that is five-sixths shared records the sixth and abandons the rest.

- 2026-08-31: **the per-assistant spending cap was configured NOWHERE in the fleet, so the control and the fix that had just been made to it were exercised by no origin that boots (T-154).** `Executor#enforce_spending_cap!` returns at its first line without `config.spending_cap`, and a measurement over every demo and the e2e harness found one comment and no assignment — which is precisely why a cap an assistant could defeat by alternating the capitalisation of its currency sat unnoticed until K-1251 went looking. hoteling now wires the batteries-included column seam and carries a `demo:spending_cap` task that settles a stay under the cap, is refused `403 spending_cap_exceeded` on one that would cross it, and — the point — spells those two charges "eur" and "EUR", so a tally that keyed on the string would let the second through. Uncapped assistants are unaffected: the column is null until an operator writes it. Intent: a control whose only proof is a unit spec is a control nobody has watched work.
